A Graded Modal Dependent Type Theory with Erasure, Formalized
Andreas Abel, Nils Anders Danielsson, Oskar Eriksson

Abstract
. We present a graded modal type theory, a dependent type theory with grades that can be used to enforce various properties of the code. The theory has Π \Pi -types, weak and strong Σ \Sigma -types, natural numbers, an empty type, and a universe, and we also extend the theory with weak and strong unit types and graded Σ \Sigma -types. The theory is parameterized by a modality structure, a kind of partially ordered semiring, whose elements (grades) are used to track the usage of variables in terms and types. Different modalities are possible. We focus mainly on quantitative properties, in particular erasure: with the erasure modality one can mark function arguments as erasable. The theory is fully formalized in Agda. The formalization, which uses a syntactic Kripke logical relation at its core and is based on earlier work, establishes major meta-theoretic properties such as subject reduction, consistency, normalization, and decidability of definitional equality. We also prove a substitution theorem for grade assignment, and preservation of grades under reduction. Furthermore we study an extraction function that translates terms to an untyped λ \lambda -calculus and removes erasable content, in particular function arguments with the “erasable” grade. For a certain class of modalities we prove that extraction is sound, in the sense that programs of natural number type have the same value before and after extraction. Soundness of extraction holds also for open programs, as long as all variables in the context are erasable, the context is consistent, and erased matches are not allowed for weak Σ \Sigma -types.

0000-0003-0420-4492

0000-0001-8688-0333

0009-0003-9505-4545

1.
Introduction

Some strongly typed programming languages come with static analyses that determine how variables are used in an expression.
The results of these analyses may contribute to the decision of whether a program is accepted,
or they might inform compiler optimizations or give extra guarantees about accepted programs.
Some of these analyses are:

(1)

Variance:

Rocq
(
rocq:900
)
and Agda
(
agda:280
)
have positivity checking for (co)inductive types,
and Scala has variance checking for higher-order subtyping
(
DBLP:phd/dnb/Steffen99
;
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/StuckiG21
)
.

(2)

Irrelevance:

Agda allows function arguments to be declared proof-irrelevant
(
pfenning:intextirr
;
abelScherer:types10
)
.

(3)

Erasure:

Agda and Idris
(
DBLP:conf/ecoop/Brady21
)
allow function arguments to be declared irrelevant for execution,
hence erasable during compilation
(
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/Tejiscak20
)
.

(4)

Linearity:

In Haskell
(
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/BernardyBNJS18
)
and Idris
(
DBLP:conf/ecoop/Brady21
)
variables can be declared to be linear.

Both erasure and linearity are supported by
Quantitative Type
Theory
(QTT)
(
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
)
, in which a semiring of
quantities is used to keep track of how variables are used.
QTT is based on earlier work by
DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
,
and there is related work in simply-typed settings by
DBLP:conf/esop/BrunelGMZ14
,
ghicaSmith:esop14
and
orchard:icfp14
.
It has further been observed that information-flow analyses fit the same framework,
by viewing the lattice of security levels as a partially ordered semiring

(
10.1145/2951913.2951939
;
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/OrchardLE19
;
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/AbelB20
;
DBLP:conf/esop/ChoudhuryEW22
)
.
We use the term
graded modal type theory

(
DBLP:conf/esop/MoonEO21
)
for a type theory with grades taken from a semiring of some kind.

In this work we study a graded modal type theory with full-fledged dependent types, i.e.,
\Pi
- and
\Sigma
-types,
a universe, and a type of natural numbers that permits large elimination.
We follow
abel:types18
and
DBLP:conf/esop/MoonEO21
in allowing resources to be tracked in types in addition to in terms (but we do not require such tracking).
The type theory comes with a meta-theory, including soundness of
substitution, subject reduction, normalization, and decidability of
definitional equality, proved via logical relations.
The meta-theory is fully formalized in Agda: we build on an Agda
formalization originally due to
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18
.

Our framework supports quantitative instances such as erasure and linearity, and information-flow instances.
However, it does not support instances that affect the definitional equality, like (compile-time) irrelevance.

Different instances of the framework lead to different guarantees for accepted programs.
For example, quantitative instances can enable certain runtime optimizations,
like deallocation of a linear resource after the first access

(
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
;
choudhuryEadesEisenbergWeirich:popl21
;
eriksson:lic
)
.
In this article, we focus on instances that allow erasure interpretations and show that actual erasure of parts marked as erasable does not alter program behaviour.
As opposed to syntactic arguments based on reduction relations

(
DBLP:conf/fossacs/Mishra-LingerS08
;
DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
;
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/Tejiscak20
)
,
we employ a logical relation between our type theory and an untyped target language.
This soundness proof is also fully formalized in Agda,
resting on the formalized meta-theory to define the logical relation by recursion on types.

The novelty in our formal treatment of erasure is that we are not only considering closed programs,
but also
open
programs as long as all their free variables are marked as erasable, and,
importantly, that the open programs live in consistent contexts.
The empty type is inhabited in inconsistent contexts, so in that setting
impossible branches in programs cannot be ruled out.
Several implementations of type theory support the compilation of programs that contain axioms.
For instance, Rocq allows the compilation of programs that contain “logical axioms”, and the manual warns that “inconsistent logical axioms may lead to incorrect or non-terminating extracted terms”
(
rocq:900
)
.
We prove formally that it is fine to use consistent axioms, as long as the axioms only appear in erasable positions.

We support two variants of our framework, one with and one without

erased matches
for weak
\Sigma
-types: if such matches are allowed
then matching on an erased pair is permitted if the obtained
components are only used in erased positions.
Erased matches are akin to Rocq’s
singleton elimination
for single-constructor propositions,
and are allowed in some graded modal type theories
(
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/Tejiscak20
;
DBLP:conf/esop/MoonEO21
;
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/AbelB20
)

but disallowed in others
(
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
;
choudhuryEadesEisenbergWeirich:popl21
;
DBLP:conf/esop/ChoudhuryEW22
)
.
Our proof of soundness of extraction does not work for open programs
if erased matches are allowed, but we suspect that soundness could be
proved also in this case (see
).

Following
abel:types18
and
DBLP:conf/esop/MoonEO21
we
annotate a
\Pi
-type with
two
grades: one is used for terms of
that type, whereas the other is used for the codomain of the

\Pi
-type.
We do not make active use of the feature in this text, and our results should hold also for a system without it.
However, we believe
that it could be useful for some type theories where types are
relevant for computation, for instance due to the presence of

type-case

(
DBLP:journals/jfp/CraryWM02
)
, or for some
variants of cubical type theory

(
cohenCoquandHuberMortberg:cubical
)
.

We make the following contributions:

(1)

We extend the ordered semiring of grades with operations and laws used for the recursor for natural numbers
(
Section

3
), refining previous work that used star-semirings to model recursion
(
DBLP:conf/esop/BrunelGMZ14
)
.

(2)

In
Section

4
we present a graded modal type theory with large eliminations that allows separate usage tracking for terms and types

(
abel:types18
;
DBLP:conf/esop/MoonEO21
)
, with a fully formalized meta-theory.
To the best of our knowledge, this is the first full formalization of the meta-theory
of a graded dependent type theory with a universe and large eliminations.

(3)

Section

5
contributes a grading judgement for which we prove meta-theoretical properties like substitution and preservation under reduction akin to the work of
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-2005-02247
. The judgement classifies usage of free variables in expressions and is decidable.

(4)

In
Section

6
we consider the special case of erasability accounting and give a formalized proof of the correctness of erasure using a logical relation.

(5)

A discussion of our design choices and alternatives follows in
,
in particular concerning the grading of the recursor for natural numbers,
but also erased matches and unit types.

(6)

In
we consider an extension by
graded
\Sigma
-types
along the lines of
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
and
choudhuryEadesEisenbergWeirich:popl21

that allows us to model record types with erased fields.
This extension has been formalized as well.

This paper is an extension of our article published at ICFP 2023
(
icfp-version
)
.
For the reader familiar with this work we highlight the main additions below:

(1)

The natrec-star operator that was previously used to assign grades to the eliminator for natural numbers has been replaced with a new, more flexible operation “
\mathsf{nr}
functions”.
The motivation behind this change is to avoid issues with expressing e.g.linearity using natrec-star.
We discuss this issue further in
as well as how to define (what we believe to be) suitable
\mathsf{nr}
functions for some instances.

(2)

In our erasure case study (
section

6
) we now target both a strict (call-by-value) and a non-strict (call-by-name) backend as opposed to only a non-strict one.
The latter now uses a more aggressive extraction function that removes both erased lambdas and function arguments as opposed to just replacing arguments with a “dummy” term.
In addition, some dead code is now extracted to a looping term instead of a dummy term to highlight that it is not evaluated.

(3)

The technical presentation of the erasure logical relation in
section

6
is simplified thanks to a new logical relation for types over the previous reducibility logical relation.

(4)

The weak and strong unit types that were speculatively discussed have now been implemented in the formalization, see
.

Further, the usage inference and decision procedures that were previously only detailed in an appendix have been incorporated into
section

5
.

Our formalization
(
formalization
)
is a fork of a formalization of decidability of type conversion originally due to
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18
.
Definitions and theorems in this paper link to their formalization in an HTML rendering of the
Agda code
.
Such links are marked in
blue
.

Note that there are differences between the presentation in the
text and the formalization.
For one, there is a single formalization
with parameters that make it possible to control whether the theory
should include things like erased matches for weak
\Sigma
-types
(
), units types
(
), graded
\Sigma
-types with or without

\eta
-equality and with particular grades (
), and one
or two modes (
).
Another difference is that the formalization uses well-scoped syntax,
whereas the text does not.
In order to aid readability we do not include side conditions related
to things like sizes of contexts in the text.

In the following section, aimed at domain experts, we locate our work more precisely in the context of contemporary research.
This section may be skipped at first reading, the technical content starts in
Section

3
.

2.
Relation to the state of the art

Our work is a contribution to an active research field about
modalities and type systems. We contribute to a corner of the
field where modalities form semirings, or, as a
special case for information-flow analyses, lattices of
security levels. There are other conceptions of modalities,
such as adjoint intuitionistic linear logics with split contexts
(
DBLP:conf/lics/CervesatoP96
;
krishnaswamiPradicBenton:popl15
;
DBLP:conf/fossacs/Vakar15
)
,
“Fitch-style” multi-modal type theories
(
DBLP:journals/lmcs/GratzerKNB21
)
,
where modalities are morphisms
between modes, or (idempotent, monadic) modalities in homotopy type theory
(
DBLP:journals/lmcs/RijkeSS19
)
, which are type functors that satisfy certain properties.

In the following we locate our work more precisely in the design/feature
space by presenting and motivating some design decisions.

2.1.
Form of judgement: separate and one-sided sage accounting

Our central judgement is
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
where
t
is an expression
(term or type) and
\gamma
is a
usage context
assigning each
free variable in
t
a grade
p
. We separate this judgement from
the usual typing judgement
\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
that expresses that

t
has type
B
in typing context
\Gamma
which assigns a type to
each variable that is in scope for
t
and
B
.

Alternatively, we could have considered a single judgement

\Phi\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
where
\Phi=\gamma\Gamma
is a context mapping variables to
pairs
pA
of their type
A
and their grade
p
. This is the
dominant conception in the literature

(
pfenning:intextirr
;
reedPierce:icfp10
;
orchard:icfp14
;
ghicaSmith:esop14
;
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/BernardyBNJS18
;
choudhuryEadesEisenbergWeirich:popl21
)
.
For simple type systems, this view is equivalent to ours,
since there in the judgement
\Phi\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B

grades only apply to the term
t
,
not to the type
B
that does not mention any of the variables declared in
\Phi
.

However in dependent type systems, where
\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
usually
implies
\Gamma\vdash{}B\mathrel{:}\mathsf{Type}
, the separation becomes conceptually
important, because
\Phi\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
may often not imply
\Phi\vdash{}B\mathrel{:}\mathsf{Type}
. It does so
1
1

1

pfenning:intextirr
’s Lemma 2 states this in a form we would write
as
\Phi\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}qB
but this form is essentially an
abbreviation for what we would write as

\Phi/q\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
with a “left-divided” context
\Phi/q
he
writes as
\Phi^{\oplus/\ominus}
.
e.g. in
pfenning:intextirr
’s
modal type theory of intensionality, extensionality and proof
irrelevance
(
pfenning:intextirr
)
,
but it does not necessarily do so for quantitative modalities
that denote how often a variable is used in an expression.
2
2

2

For instance, in
x(A)\vdash{}\mathsf{refl}\,x\mathrel{:}\mathsf{Eq}\,x\,x
,
the term mentions
x
once while its type does so twice.

DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
pioneered an approach in which
terms
t
and their types
B
are considered in two different
worlds:
t
in a “material” world where the resources
\gamma
for

t
are consumed by the construction of
t
, and
B
in a ‘‘mental’’
world where resources do not matter.
3
3

3

McBride uses the words “consumption” and “contemplation” for what we paraphrased here as “material” and “mental”.

Consequently, they introduced a
grade
0
for “no consumption” and a judgement we would write as

\Phi\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}0B
.
The latter is essentially bare typing
\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
and

\Phi
is there of the form
\boldsymbol{0}\Gamma
, i.e. resource-free.
Their typing rules are formulated via a
two-sided
judgement
\Phi\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}qB
,
where both the hypotheses and the conclusion are annotated with grades
drawn from a “rig” (a ri
n
g without
n
egation), in particular

\{0,1,\omega\}
(‘‘none-one-tons’’).
4
4

4

Quantity
0
for no use (“none”),
1
for a single use (“one”), and
\omega
for unrestricted use (“tons”).

The judgement
\gamma\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}qB
was to mean, paraphrased, that “to produce
q
copies of the output
t
we need
\gamma(x)
copies of each input
(x:A)\in\Gamma
”.
In that sense, grades may also be called
multiplicities
.

The two-sided form
\Phi\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}qB
has been considered for information flow tracking

(
volpano:jcs96
;
DBLP:conf/esop/ChoudhuryEW22
)
,
where
q
is the security level of the output and
\Phi
declares the security levels of the inputs.
However, as
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
observed
(
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
)
,

DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
’s calculus failed the substitution property for
q=\omega
(unrestricted use).

DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
fixed this by giving up the general “
q
copies” intuition
and restricting the right-hand side quantity
q
to
\{0,1\}

where the unit
1
represents the “material”, resource-aware world and
0
the “mental”, purely logical one.

DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
’s formulation is equivalent to splitting the judgement into a one-sided one,
\gamma\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
for world
1
, and a non-resourced one,
\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
for world
0
.
From here, we proceed and separate resourcing/usage into its own judgement
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
.
This works for us because we ensure that
t
contains enough annotations such that this judgement can be defined without reference to types.
However, note that some of
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
’s term formers may only be used in the non-resourced setting, for instance the projections for his dependent tensor products.
The corresponding
\beta
- and
\eta
-rules are also only present in the non-resourced setting.
Our system does not support this (see also
section

2.3
).

In
we consider a variant
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{m}t
, which resembles Atkey’s two-world accounting.
In that setting we support graded
\Sigma
-types, which are similar to Atkey’s dependent tensors.
However, instead of having a single family of types which is “strong” in the non-resourced setting and “weak” otherwise, we have one variant which is always strong and one which is always weak:
the first projection for the strong graded
\Sigma
-type
\Sigma^{q}_{\&,0}\,F\,G
is only available in the erased setting, but the
\eta
-rule for this type is always active.
We discuss under what conditions
\eta
-expansion is resource-preserving for the strong graded
\Sigma
-types.

DBLP:journals/lmcs/FuKS22
present a linear dependent type theory for quantum programming with a base type for qubits which is subject to linearity.
Their solution for the coexistence of linearity and dependency is different:
Expressions occurring in types must belong to the “intuitionistic fragment” of the language,
in particular not contain qubits or other entities that must adhere to the linearity discipline.
Whenever an expression travels from the term- to the type-side of a judgement, such as in the
\Pi
-elimination rule, its linear parts are erased by the Sh(-) operation.
The intuition is that Sh(-), stripping the qubits, only leaves the
shape
of a value.
Integrating an erasure operation into the typing rules is beyond the capabilities of our system.

2.2.
Usage accounting also in types

We take a further step by allowing (but not requiring) an independent usage declaration
\delta\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}B
for the type
B
of
t
, rather than ignoring variable usage in
B
.
This has been pioneered by
abel:types18
and
DBLP:conf/esop/MoonEO21
. The latter maintain a triple
(\Delta,\gamma,\delta)
in the typing judgement
\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
, where
\gamma
and
\delta
are the resource vectors of
t
and
B
, respectively, and
\Delta
is a triangular resource matrix for
\Gamma
, declaring how each hypothesis in
\Gamma
depends resource-wise on the previous hypotheses.
The full potential of usage accounting in types has not been explored yet, and we do not contribute such applications in this work either. However,
DBLP:conf/esop/MoonEO21
presented some experiments on optimizing type-checking based on usage annotations in types.

There is another reason why we account for usage also in types:
ignoring usage in types—or at least in terms that stand for types—can be unsound in extensions of type theory with aspects of homotopy type theory or cubical type theory (CTT).

abel-danielsson-vezzosi-erased-univalence
discuss a variant of CTT with support for erasure.
They present examples showing that if the rules for erasure are not set up properly, then one can construct two booleans that are provably distinct but equal after erasure.
The examples do not rely on CTT, it suffices to have an
erased
univalence axiom
(
hott-book
)
.

The usage rules that we present for the
\Pi
and
\Sigma
type constructors in
are similar to those presented by
abel-danielsson-vezzosi-erased-univalence
, with one important difference: our type constructors are annotated with
two
grades instead of one.
However, our formalization is parameterized by a
relation
between those two grades
which in particular makes it possible to force them to be equal.

2.3.
Semantics refines resource-free semantics

We maintain the standard definitional equality judgement
\Gamma\vdash{}t=t^{\prime}\mathrel{:}B
of type theory.
This means that modalities do not interact with equality, limiting the expressiveness of our framework.
For instance, it cannot model proof irrelevance
(
pfenning:intextirr
;
abelScherer:types10
;
DBLP:conf/esop/ChoudhuryEW22
;
liu:indistinguishability
)

or variance
(
DBLP:phd/dnb/Steffen99
;
abel:csr06
;
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/StuckiG21
)
—the latter is an unsolved problem for dependent types.
However, this limitation allows us to employ a standard semantics of dependent types and terms,
ignoring grade annotations at first.
The resource-aware semantics is then a
refinement
of that first semantics,
faithful to our separation of the usage relation
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
from the typing relation
\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
.
Such a two-phase approach has been employed by
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18

who first gives a standard
categories with families
(CwF) semantics to the types and terms of his Quantitative Type Theory (QTT),
and then refines CwFs to
quantitative CwFs
to prove resource-correctness of terms.
In our case, the resource-free semantics is given by a
reducibility
logical relation following
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18
, and we refine it for erasure instances by an
erasure
logical relation defined over reducible types.

With simple types, well-typed terms
x_{1}(p)_{1}A_{1},\dots,x_{n}(p)_{1}A_{n}\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}B
in the one-sided judgement
can be interpreted as morphisms

\llbracket t\rrbracket:D^{p_{1}}\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket\times\dots\times D^{p_{n}}\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket\to\llbracket B\rrbracket
for a
graded
comonad
D

(
ghicaSmith:esop14
;
orchard:icfp14
)
.
Thus it is natural to refer to the semiring elements
p_{i}
as
grades
.
We adopt this terminology even if we do not see how this semantics formally extends to dependent types in the general case.

2.4.
Formalization

We have fully formalized our results in Agda
(
agda:280
)
.
A prime motivation for having mechanized proofs is correctness.
It has already been mentioned that the substitution property failed in the work of
DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
.
With a mechanized formalization one can also build on and change previous work without knowing every detail of every proof: if anything breaks, then one is informed of this (barring any bugs in proof checkers).
This has been a big benefit as we have been extending our original work on the formalization
(
icfp-version
)
.
Our formalization started out as a fork of the formalization originally due to
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18
,
5
5

5

Our fork is based on commit
bb69ad3f39
, which includes further developments, some by Oskar Eriksson, Gaëtan Gilbert and Wojciech Nawrocki.

and our formalization could in turn be the starting point for further work.

2.5.
Universes and large eliminations

Our development includes a universe and a recursor for natural numbers that can compute values and types,
the latter being known as
large elimination
.
Thus we ensure that our results are general enough and scale to full-fledged dependent types.
This generality eliminates common short-cuts in establishing the meta-theory.
For instance,
DBLP:conf/esop/MoonEO21
prove strong normalization via an embedding of reduction into a non-dependent polymorphic language,
adapting the work of
geuvers:shortFlexibleSNCC
.
Such techniques are not available with large eliminations; we instead
pursue the more stony path of dependent logical relations outlined by
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18
.
However, this gives us a meta-theory with subject reduction, normalization, and decidability of judgemental equality, via a typed
reducibility
logical relation.

DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
presents a more extensional semantics using quantitative CwFs, which also lends itself to large elimination.
This semantics is used to show that linear execution (BCI algebras with some extra structure) forms a model of QTT.
A reviewer of our ICFP 2023 paper
(
icfp-version
)
suggested that one could also derive meta-theoretic results like decidability from a suitable CwF, however, as far as we know this has not been spelled out.

2.6.
Instance: erasure, justified by a logical relation

In
Section

6
we introduce the
erasure
lattice
\omega\leq 0
(retained vs. erased) as a possible instance of our generic graded type theory.
The same instance can also be understood as the 2-point security lattice
\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{H}
(public vs. private).

We demonstrate that erasure of
0
-annotated function arguments is sound, i.e., preserves canonical forms at base type,
via an ‘‘erasure’’ logical relation indexed by a logical relation for types.
6
6

6

A reviewer of
icfp-version
pointed out that a similar result could perhaps be obtained for QTT by instantiating its realizability semantics
(
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
, Section 4)
so that
!_{p}x
is a dummy for
p=0
.

There is also the syntactic path to correctness of erasure,
by showing that reduction is simulated in the target language

(
letouzey:types02
;
DBLP:conf/fossacs/Mishra-LingerS08
;
DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
;
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/Tejiscak20
)
.
The syntactic approach does not rely on normalization or semantics and can thus scale to non-terminating languages.
However, statements like “if a source term has a value, then the corresponding target term has a value” may not be enough: it could be the case that a source term does not have a value, and still the corresponding target term has a value.
In the presence of
erased matches
that can be the case for an open term in a consistent, erasable context (see
).
We do not solve this problem, but we discuss it.

choudhuryEadesEisenbergWeirich:popl21
consider a quantitative dependent type theory
GraD

(without universes) with a heap semantics.
They prove that quantities correctly predict the number of run-time accesses to heap-stored variables
by giving a reference-counting reduction semantics.
To show that reductions preserve typing, they extend
GraD
by definitions in the sense of delayed substitutions.
It could be worthwhile to investigate whether a (Kripke) logical relation could be utilized for the heap soundness proof of the original
GraD
, without delayed substitutions.

3.
Modalities organized as ordered semirings

We begin by giving the definition of modalities via semiring-like structures.
The definition largely follows that of
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/AbelB20
but is also based on
DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
’s rig for erasure and linearity
(
DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
)
, its extension by
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
, and the quantitative coeffect calculus of
DBLP:conf/esop/BrunelGMZ14
, all of which use similar algebraic structures.

Definition 3.1 (Modality structure)
.

A modality structure is a 7-tuple

(\mathcal{M},+,\cdot,\wedge,\mathsf{nr},0,1)
,
consisting of (from left to right)
a type
\mathcal{M}
of
modalities
or
grades
,
three binary operations (addition, multiplication, and meet),
a 5-ary function
\mathsf{nr}
,
and zero and unit elements,
satisfying the following properties
7
7

7

Here, and elsewhere, we occasionally colourize some grades for highlighting.
:

•

(\mathcal{M},+,\cdot,0,1)
forms a semiring: Addition is commutative and associative with
0
as identity. Multiplication is associative with
1
as identity and
0
as absorbing element and is distributive over addition.
Like
DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16

we do
not
require
0\not=1
, thus,
the unit type is a valid (but trivial) modality structure.

As usual, we will typically abbreviate multiplication of
p
and
q
as
pq
.

•

(\mathcal{M},\wedge)
forms a semilattice: Meet is commutative, associative, and idempotent. The semilattice induces the usual partial ordering relation:
p\leq q
iff
p=p\wedge q
.

•

Both multiplication and addition distribute over meet.

•

The function
\mathsf{nr}
for the grading of
\mathbb{N}
-recursion satisfies the following properties (for all grades
p
,
r
,
q_{i}
, and
q_{i}^{\prime}
):

–

Base: If
{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq 0
then
\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}
.

–

Step:
\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}\cdot\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}

–

\mathsf{nr}
is monotone in its last three arguments, i.e., if
{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{z}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{s}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{n}}}
then
\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{n}}}
.

–

Multiplication is right sub-distributive over the last three arguments of
\mathsf{nr}
, i.e.,
(\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}})\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}\leq\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,({\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q})\,({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q})\,({\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q})

–

Addition is sub-interchangeable with the last three arguments of
\mathsf{nr}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}+\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,({\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{z}}})\,({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{s}}})\,({\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q^{\prime}_{\mathrm{n}}})
.

•

Equality is decidable. This property is used in
decision procedures for typing and usage as well as to differentiate between
0
and other grades in the erasure case study.

We will typically use
\mathcal{M}
to refer both to the modality structure and the underlying type.
As we will see, the elements of
\mathcal{M}
are used to give modal interpretations to programs and, in particular, to free variables.
Which interpretations are available is determined by the specific modality structure that is used.

The operators represent different ways of combining interpretations.

Addition
combines grades of subterms when both are used, for instance
for components of a weak pair, and
meet
when only one is used, for
instance for components of a strong pair or for branches in a case
distinction.

Multiplication
allows us to scale by the number of uses.
The operator
\mathsf{nr}
, which we will refer to as the “
\mathsf{nr}
function” of the modality structure, has a more specific use and will be used to combine grades for use with the natural number eliminator.
We discuss its use, as well as the roles of the five arguments, when we use it to define grade assignment for
\mathsf{natrec}
in
section

5
.
The
partial order
encodes a form of compatibility between grades.
Intuitively,
p\leq q
can be interpreted as allowing
p
to be used in place of
q
.
Note that from a quantitative perspective, the direction of the order relation is different from what one might first expect since a smaller grade represents a resource that is allowed to be used
more
times.

The properties of modalities are used for our proofs of the grade analogues of subject reduction and the substitution lemma.
For instance, the (sub-) distributivity properties for meet ensure that
addition
and
multiplication
are monotone operations.
For the most part, this definition thus matches the one of
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/AbelB20
with the main exception being the
\mathsf{nr}
function, which is used for
\mathsf{natrec}
.

Following
orchard:icfp14
and

DBLP:journals/pacmpl/AbelB20

we introduce grade or
usage contexts
, analogous to typing contexts,
that map free variables to grades.
For contexts of the same domain, the operators are
lifted to act pointwise
in the following way:

\displaystyle(\gamma+\delta)(x)

\displaystyle=\gamma(x)+\delta(x)

\displaystyle(p\cdot\gamma)(x)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\gamma(x)

\displaystyle(\gamma\wedge\delta)(x)

\displaystyle=\gamma(x)\wedge\delta(x)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{nr}_{p}^{r}\,\gamma\,\delta\,\eta)(x)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{nr}_{p}^{r}\,\gamma(x)\,\delta(x)\,\eta(x)

Note that the lifting of
\mathsf{nr}
is done only over the last three arguments while the first two remain single grades, and the left argument of multiplication is also a single grade.
Since the operators are defined pointwise, all properties can similarly be lifted to also hold for contexts.
Thus, usage contexts of a given domain
form
a
left semimodule
over the semiring, with the zero context,
\mathbf{0}
, as zero
(
DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
)
.
The ordering relation is
lifted pointwise
as well, so that
\gamma\leq\delta
iff
\gamma(x)\leq\delta(x)
for all
x
in the domain of
\gamma
and
\delta
, or equivalently,
\gamma\wedge\delta=\gamma
.

The already mentioned
trivial
(one element) modality structure which reduces the system to the underlying non-graded system is a valid instance.
Below we list some more interesting instances.
For all of them it is possible to define lawful definitions of
\mathsf{nr}
, however, we mostly defer the discussion of this to
.

\Description

Operator definitions for some modality structures.

Figure 1.
Operator definitions for some modality structures.

Erasure
.

The two-element set
\{0,\omega\}
with
0
as the additive unit,
\omega
as the multiplicative unit, operators as defined in
fig.

1
is a valid modality structure. Note that addition and meet coincide.
The partial order induced by the semilattice is the reflexive closure of
0\leq\omega
.
For this instance, the
only lawful definition
of
\mathsf{nr}
is
\mathsf{nr}_{p}^{r}\,q_{z}\,q_{s}\,q_{n}=q_{z}\wedge q_{s}\wedge q_{n}
, independent of
p
and
r
.
A possible interpretation of this instance is erasure which is the focus of our case study in
section

6
, with
0
denoting computationally irrelevant information and
\omega
denoting computationally relevant information.
But the same instance can also be used for information flow in the lattice
\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{H}

(see
).

Affine Types
.

The three-element set
\{0,1,\omega\}
with
0
as the additive unit,
1
as the multiplicative unit and operators as defined in
fig.

1
has the intended interpretation of affine types.
This interpretation is supported by the partial order,
\omega\leq 1\leq 0
, where
1
being smaller than
0
allows interpreting the grade
1
as representing either one or zero uses.

Linear Types
.

The same instance with the meet operator changed such that
1\geq\omega\leq 0
instead gives the intended interpretation of linear types.
Since there is no grade smaller than
1
, this grade is now interpreted as exactly one use.

Linear & Affine Types
.

The linear and affine types interpretations can be combined using the four-element set
\{0,1,1^{\text{?}},\omega\}
,
where
1
and
1^{\text{?}}
represent linear and affine uses, respectively.
The operators are defined in
fig.

1
and the partial order is given by
\omega\leq 1^{\text{?}}
and
1\geq 1^{\text{?}}\leq 0
.

Bounded, Distributive Lattices
.

Bounded, distributive lattices with decidable equality can be turned into modality structures where addition is meet and multiplication is join,
the top element is the unit of addition (
0
), and the bottom element is the unit of multiplication (
1
).
As for the two-point lattice,
\mathsf{nr}_{p}^{r}\,q_{z}\,q_{s}\,q_{n}=q_{z}\wedge q_{s}\wedge q_{n}

is a valid
\mathsf{nr}
function
.
Lattices are used in information flow applications, which we discuss further in
.

4.
Type theory with grades

The language we consider,
\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}
, is an extension of the language originally used in the formalization by
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18
.
Its syntax is shown below.
The language includes the single (Russell) universe
\mathsf{U}
, natural number type
\mathbb{N}
, and
\Pi
-type of the original formalization by
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18
,
as well as the later additions of
an empty type (
\bot
) and strong
\Sigma
-types.
8
8

8

The empty
type was added to the formalization by Gaëtan Gilbert in 2018. Wojciech Nawrocki added strong
\Sigma
-types and a unit type in 2021. The unit type is discussed in
.

This work further adds weak
\Sigma
-types.
The formalization also supports identity types and a hierarchy of universes
9
9

9

The identity type was added by Nils Anders Danielsson, and the universe hierarchy by Nils Anders Danielsson and Ondřej Kubánek.
but we do not discuss these.

Since different modality structures
\mathcal{M}
give different interpretations,
\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}
is more accurately seen as a family of languages ranging over the possible structures.

4.1.
Syntax

Expressions (terms and types) are given by the following grammar:

\displaystyle\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Untyped.html#Term\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}\;|\;\mathbb{N}\;|\;\bot\;|\;\Pi^{q}_{p}\,A\,B\;|\;\Sigma^{q}_{\&}\,A\,B\;|\;\Sigma^{q}_{\otimes}\,A\,B

\displaystyle\;|\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{i}\;|\;\lambda^{p}t\;|\;t\,^{p}u\;|\;(t,u)_{\&}\;|\;\mathsf{fst}{}\,t\;|\;\mathsf{snd}{}\,t\;|\;(t,u)_{\otimes}\;|\;\mathsf{prodrec}{}_{r}^{q}\,A\,t\,u

\displaystyle\;|\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{zero}\;|\;\mathsf{suc}{}\,t\;|\;\mathsf{natrec}{}_{p,r}^{q}\,A\,t\,u\,v\;|\;\mathsf{emptyrec}_{p}\,A\,t\ignorespaces

Note that there are two kinds of
\Sigma
-types and pair constructors, annotated with
\&
and
\otimes
for strong and weak
\Sigma
-types, respectively.
Often we will not need to differentiate between the two and simply write
\Sigma^{q}\,A\,B
, or
\Sigma^{q}_{k}\,A\,B
, and similarly for pairs.
We include two kinds of
\Sigma
-types because the types have different characteristics: the strong kind comes with projections and
\eta
-equality while the weak one comes with pattern matching (
\mathsf{prodrec}
) and optionally erased matches (and Agda supports all of these variants).

We use de Bruijn-style nameless syntax and write
\mathsf{x}_{i}
for the variable with index
i
.
The subterms which bind new variables are the second argument
B
to
\Pi
and
\Sigma
-types, the body
t
of lambda abstractions, and the first argument
A
to
\mathsf{natrec}
and
\mathsf{prodrec}
which each bind one variable, as well as the third arguments
u
to
\mathsf{natrec}
and
\mathsf{prodrec}
which bind two variables.
Since the original formalization
(
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18
)
the formalized language has been updated to be well-scoped by construction, so subterms are guaranteed to not introduce more variables than specified.

Some terms are annotated with grades, indicated by
p
,
q
, and
r
.
These denote elements from the set of modalities and are used to give programs their desired interpretations based on the chosen instance.
The meaning of an annotation varies somewhat between terms and will be detailed in
Section

5
.

4.2.
Weakening and substitution

Weakenings

\rho
are constructed from the identity weaking
\mathsf{id}
by adding shiftings
{\uparrow}
and liftings
{\Uparrow}
. Identity (
\mathsf{id}
) leaves terms unchanged, shifting (
{\uparrow}
) increases variable indices by one, and lifting (
{\Uparrow}
) is used when a weakening is pushed under a binder.
The application of a weakening
\rho
to a term
t
is denoted by
{t[\rho]}
.
For variables we have
{\mathsf{x}_{i}[\rho]}=\mathsf{x}_{{i[\rho]}}
, with the following definition of
{i[\rho]}
:

\displaystyle{i[\mathsf{id}]}

\displaystyle=i

\displaystyle{i[{\uparrow}\rho]}

\displaystyle={i[\rho]}+1

\displaystyle{0[{\Uparrow}\rho]}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{(i+1)[{\Uparrow}\rho]}

\displaystyle={i[\rho]}+1

For other terms the weakening is applied to each subterm, lifted
n
times for subterms in which
n
new variables are bound.

Substitutions
are functions from variable indices to terms.
The weakening constructors above can be implemented for substitutions, and we overload the notation.
We also use the notation
{t[\sigma]}
for the application of a substitution
\sigma
to a term
t
.
This operation can be implemented similarly to the application of weakenings, with the variable case
{\mathsf{x}_{i}[\sigma]}=\sigma(i)
.
Given a substitution
\sigma
and a term
t
one can form the
extended
substitution
\sigma,t
satisfying

(\sigma,t)(0)=t
and
(\sigma,t)(i+1)=\sigma(i)
.
Note that the substitution
(\mathsf{id},u)
replaces
\mathsf{x}_{0}
with
u
and reduces the indices of all other free variables by one.
To avoid clutter we will often drop the identity substitution.
For instance, we shall write
{t[u]}
instead of
{t[\mathsf{id},u]}

and
{t[{\uparrow}]}
instead of
{t[{\uparrow}\mathsf{id}]}
.
Given a substitution
\sigma
we let
\mathsf{head}\,\sigma=\sigma(0)
and
(\mathsf{tail}\,\sigma)(i)=\sigma(i+1)
.
Note that
\mathsf{head}\,(\sigma,t)=t
and
\mathsf{tail}\,(\sigma,t)=\sigma
.

4.3.
Typing and equality

\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}
is dependently typed with the typical judgements:

\vdash{}\Gamma
for well-formed contexts,

\Gamma\vdash{}A
for well-formed types,

\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}A
for well-formed terms of type
A
and
\Gamma\vdash{}A=B
and

\Gamma\vdash{}t=u\mathrel{:}A
for type and term equality.
Their inductive definitions are given in
Figures

2
and
3
.

Contexts
are lists of types, each giving a type to a free variable.
We write
\epsilon
for the empty context and
\Gamma.A
for the context
\Gamma
extended with
A
.
In that context,
\mathsf{x}_{0}
has type
{A[{\uparrow}]}
and
\Gamma
contains type information for the remaining free variables as expressed by the relation
\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathrel{:}B\in{}\Gamma
defined in
fig.

2
.

For the most part these judgements are unchanged by the addition of grades and are defined as expected.
However, we include some premises can be proven from the others in order to simplify the meta-theory.
We mark such premises that can be dropped a posteriori with a grey background.
In some rules certain grades are required to match, for instance in the typing rule for lambda abstractions, where the annotation on the lambda is required to match one of the
\Pi
-type’s annotations.
The remaining work of ensuring correctness of grade annotations is instead handled by the usage relation, which is defined in
Section

5
.

Equality of types,
\Gamma\vdash{}A=B
, and terms,

Figure 3.
Equality of types,
\Gamma\vdash{}A=B
, and terms,
\Gamma\vdash{}t=u\mathrel{:}A
.

One can also note the differences between weak and strong
\Sigma
-types.
Some rules are shared between the two variants, while some only apply to one of them.
In particular, the rules involving projections—including the rule for
\eta
-equality—are only valid for strong
\Sigma
-types.
Rules involving
\mathsf{prodrec}
are only given for weak
\Sigma
-types.

We have typeset certain grade and
\Sigma
-type annotations in colour to highlight some non-standard aspects of the rules.

Remark 1

(Atkey’s Dependent Tensor Product Types)
.

Our
\Sigma
-types differ from the
dependent tensor product types

(x:^{p}S)\otimes T
of
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
.
In Atkey’s case, the grade
p
is the multiplicity
of the first component of the
pair
,
whereas in our case this multiplicity is always
1
,
and the
q
in
\Sigma^{q}_{k}\,F\,G
is the grade
of the first component in the
type

G
.
Note that
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
,
while including syntax and rules for the dependent tensor product,
does not spell out its semantics in the given quantitative CwF model.
We describe a variant of our theory with support for something
similar to the tensor products of

DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
—inspired by his work—in
.

Remark 2

(Eliminator for
\Sigma_{\&}
, Projections for
\Sigma_{\otimes}
)
.

One can define a variant of
\mathsf{prodrec}
for strong
\Sigma
-types by

\mathsf{prodrec}{}\,t\,u={u[\mathsf{fst}{}\,t,\mathsf{snd}{}\,t]}
,
and one can derive a
typing rule
for this construction which is similar to the one for weak
\Sigma
-types.
However, this construction does
not
in general satisfy the usage rule that we give for weak
\Sigma
-types in
section

5
.
One can also define projections for weak
\Sigma
-types using
\mathsf{prodrec}
, but
\eta
-equality does
not
hold in general for those projections.

4.4.
Reduction

The operational semantics of
\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}
is given by call-by-name weak head reduction relations.
