\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow{}B
and

\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow{}u\mathrel{:}A
(see
Figure

4
),
as well as their reflexive transitive closures,

\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}B
and

\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{*}u\mathrel{:}A
,
are typed, following
types2016decidability
;
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18
.
Terms that are in weak head normal form (WHNF) do not reduce, and reduction to WHNF is deterministic.

Theorem 4.1 (whnfs do not reduce)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{*}u\mathrel{:}A
with
t
in WHNF, then
t=u
.
Similarly, if
\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}B
with
A
in WHNF, then
A=B
.

Theorem 4.2 (whnf is unique)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{*}u\mathrel{:}A
and
\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{*}u^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A^{\prime}
with
u
and
u^{\prime}
in WHNF, then
u=u^{\prime}
.
Likewise, if
\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}B
and
\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}B^{\prime}
with
B
and
B^{\prime}
in WHNF, then
B=B^{\prime}
.

Admissibility of substitution
,

subject reduction
,

normalization
, and

decidability of equality
are proved either via well-founded induction on the typing judgements or via
a
reducibility logical relation
adapted from the work of
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18
(see the formalization for details).
Furthermore
type-checking is decidable
for a class of expressions which excludes some redexes.

5.
Assigning grades

We are now ready to explain the purpose of the different grade annotations and we will do so in parallel with introducing the usage relation which ensures that terms are correctly annotated.
Note that because the usage relation is separate from the typing relation—it is a grade assignment system—the logical relation mentioned in the previous section can be defined without reference to usage contexts.
Also note that, unlike for typing, there is only one relation for both terms and types.
Depending on the application, it might thus make sense to require
\delta\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}A
, and
\eta_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}B_{i}
for all
\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathrel{:}B_{i}\in{}\Gamma
in addition to
\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}A
and
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
(checking that the type of
t
and all types in
\Gamma
are well-resourced).

Definition 5.1 (Usage relation)
.

Given a usage context
\gamma
and term
t
,
we say that
t
is
well-resourced
with respect to
\gamma
if
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
.
That judgement is defined inductively in
fig.

5
.

\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{U}}\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathbb{N}}\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\bot}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}F\quad{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}.{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}G}{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}+{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\Pi^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}\,F\,G}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}F\quad{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}.{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}G}{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}+{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\Sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,F\,G}\frac{}{\mathbf{e}_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{x}_{i}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}.{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t}{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\lambda^{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}t}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t\quad{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}u}{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}+{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t\,^{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}u}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t\quad{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}u}{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}+{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}(t,u)_{\otimes}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t\quad{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}u}{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}(t,u)_{\&}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t}{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{fst}{}\,t}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t}{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{snd}{}\,t}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t\quad{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}.{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}.{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}u\quad\eta.{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}A}{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}+{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{prodrec}{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,t\,u}\;\mathsf{Prodrec}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}}\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{zero}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t}{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{suc}{}\,t}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma_{\mathrm{z}}}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}z\quad{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\gamma_{\mathrm{s}}}.{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}s\quad{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}n\quad\delta.{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}A}{\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\gamma_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{natrec}{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p},{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,z\,s\,n}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t\quad\delta\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}A}{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{emptyrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}\,A\,t}\;\mathsf{Emptyrec}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t}{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t}\;{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\leq{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}

\Description

The grade assignment relation
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
.

Figure 5.
The grade assignment relation
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
.

Similarly to the typing judgements of the previous section, the context is used to keep track of the grades associated with the free variables of a term.
In the explanations to follow, we will stick to a quantitative view where the grades are used to keep track of how many times some resource (term) is referenced or
used
.
It should be noted, however, that the system allows for non-quantitative interpretations as well (see
).

Starting with the simplest cases, the constants,
\mathsf{U}
,
\mathbb{N}
,
\bot
,
and
\mathsf{zero}
, are well-resourced with respect to the zero context
\mathbf{0}
.
It associates
0
with every variable in scope, none of which appears in a constant.
A variable
\mathsf{x}_{i}
is well-resourced with respect to the unit-vector context
\mathbf{e}_{i}
that maps index
i
to
1
and everything else to
0
, representing a single variable occurrence.
These rules are not as restrictive as they may first appear since the subsumption rule (last one in the figure) allows us to pass to any pointwise smaller context.
As a reminder, for quantitative modalities a smaller grade represents a resource that is allowed to be used
more
times.

For lambda abstractions
\lambda^{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}t
the annotation
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}
represents how many times the newly bound variable (the function argument) is allowed to be used in the body
t
.
Through the typing rules discussed in the previous section, this is also the meaning of
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}
in the function type
\Pi^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}\,F\,G
.
The annotation
{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}
represents how many times the function argument is allowed to be used by the co-domain
G
of the function.
Analogously, the annotation
{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}
on pair types
\Sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,F\,G
specifies how the first component is used in the type
G
of the second component.
For
\Sigma
and
\Pi
, as well as for weak pairing
(t,u)_{\otimes}
, the resulting number of uses is given by adding the uses of the subterms.
One can imagine a modality structure where the two annotations
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}
on
the
\Pi
-type should not be independent of each other, see
section

2.2
for one example.
Such cases could be handled by parameterizing typing by a suitable
relation between
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}
—and the formalization has such a parameter.

Applications
t\,^{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}u
also add the contexts of the two subterms together but with one crucial difference:
the context of the function argument
u
is first scaled by the annotation
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}
put on the application, which represents the number of times the function argument is allowed to be used.
This corresponds to the function argument possibly occurring multiple
times once
\beta
-reduction has been
performed.

In the pairing rule for weak pairs
(t,u)_{\otimes}
the contexts of the two components are simply added together as described above.
When such pairs are deconstructed via
\mathsf{prodrec}
both components are used, and the usage rule for the pair constructor accounts for both components.

The grade annotation
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}
on
\mathsf{prodrec}{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,t\,u
stands for the number of times the components of the pair can be used, and the context corresponding to the argument
t
is scaled accordingly.
The annotation
{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}
represents how many times the pair can be used in the type argument.
Note that while we check that the type argument uses resources correctly, its resources are not included in the conclusion of the rule for
\mathsf{prodrec}
, because type annotations do not contribute to computation.
The case
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}=0
represents matching on the pair without using the components, except possibly in erased positions.
An example is the program
\mathsf{prodrec}{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}0}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}0}}\,\mathbb{N}\,\mathsf{x}_{0}\,\mathsf{zero}
which matches on variable
\mathsf{x}_{0}
and returns
\mathsf{zero}
, roughly corresponding to the follow Agda code:

\@classiv
\@classz
\@classiv
\@classiv
\@classz
\@classiv
\@classiv
\@classz
\@classiv
\@classiv
\@classz
\@classiv
\@classiv
\@classz
\@classiv

\@classiv
\@classz
\@classiv
\@preamble
\@preamble

ex

:

@0

Σ

A

B

→

ℕ

\@classiv
\@classz
\@classiv
\@classiv
\@classz
\@classiv
\@preamble
\@preamble

ex

(_

,

_)

=

zero

In this case, no resources are counted from the pair (when
0\neq 1
) and we refer to this as an
erased match
.
Erased matches are discussed further in
sections

6
and
.
The configurable side condition
\mathsf{Prodrec}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}}
defines which grades
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}
may be used.
Primarily, we will use it to control whether erased matches are allowed or not.

In contrast to weak pairs, for strong pairs
(t,u)_{\&}
we instead take the
meet
of the two contexts.
Strong pairs can only be used through projections, and when a projection is used one component is discarded.
The meet of
{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}
is an approximation to both contexts.
If, for instance, a variable occurs linearly in one of the components it will only be treated linearly in the pair if it also occurs linearly in the other component.
If a variable occurs just in one component, it will be treated as affine use in the pair.
The difference between the two kinds of pairs can also be understood through the lens of linear logic
(
linearLogic
)
.
Strong pairs correspond to the multiplicative conjunction
A\otimes B
where the assumptions
\Gamma,\Delta
of both conjuncts are concatenated (
\Gamma\otimes\Delta
) when forming the pair,
while weak pairs correspond to the additive conjunction
A\&B
where both conjuncts should use the same assumptions
\Gamma
.

The eliminator for the empty type,
\mathsf{emptyrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}\,A\,t
, is similar to
\mathsf{prodrec}
.
However, because uses of this eliminator correspond to dead or impossible branches, we do not restrict the annotation
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}
(apart from the configurable side condition
\mathsf{Emptyrec}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}
), allowing the context to be scaled by any amount.
In particular,
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}=0
means that we do not count any resources.
A use of this can be found in a
safe
head
function for (sized) lists, which takes an erasable proof which shows that the list is non-empty, and uses this proof in an application of
\mathsf{emptyrec}_{0}
.
As for
\mathsf{prodrec}
, we also check the usage in the type argument
A
and the configurable side condition can be used to control which grades are allowed.

This leaves only
\mathsf{natrec}
, which
is the only term in our language with recursive and branching semantics, both of which give rise to complications.
The usage relation has primarily been defined with subject reduction in mind.
For terms with (at most) one rule for
\beta
-reduction, doing so is straightforward, but for
\mathsf{natrec}
there are two reduction alternatives and we must make sure to include enough resources for either.

This gives rise to two constraints that the context in the conclusion of the rule, call it
\chi
, should satisfy.
In the zero case,
\mathsf{natrec}{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p},{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,z\,s\,\mathsf{zero}
reduces to
z
, so for subject reduction to hold we should have
\chi\leq{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma_{\mathrm{z}}}
in this case.
Note, however, that the natural number is then the constant
\mathsf{zero}
so in this case we have
{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq\mathbf{0}
.

In the successor case,
\mathsf{natrec}{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p},{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,z\,s\,(\mathsf{suc}{}\,n)
reduces to
{s[n,\mathsf{natrec}{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p},{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,z\,s\,n]}
, which uses the resources
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\gamma_{\mathrm{s}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}\chi
(assuming a suitable substitution lemma), and this gives us the inequality
\chi\leq{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\gamma_{\mathrm{s}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}\chi
with
\chi
on both sides.

If the number of iterations, i.e., the value of
n
, is known, it would be possible to unfold the above definition and arrive at a non-implicit expression for
\chi
, but since this is not always the case we employ a different strategy.
A similar problem has been solved by
DBLP:conf/esop/BrunelGMZ14
who include a fixed-point operator and case distinction on natural numbers in their calculus
(
DBLP:conf/esop/BrunelGMZ14
)
.
Translated into our setting, their approach roughly corresponds to setting
\chi={\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}^{*}({\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma_{\mathrm{z}}}\wedge({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\gamma_{\mathrm{s}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}))
, where the unary star-operator satisfies
{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}^{*}=1+{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}^{*}
.
Using this rule, however, it seems to be difficult to prove subject reduction in general.
10
10

10

Even if subject reduction would not hold in our system with this rule, this does not mean that the system used by Brunel et al. does not have subject reduction. Our form of
\mathsf{natrec}
is not definable in their system since their rules for case branching correspond to
{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}
always being
1
in our system.

We have been unable to find a general solution to these inequalities that can be expressed using only addition, multiplication and meet.
Instead we include the function
\mathsf{nr}
which provides such a solution.
The characteristic inequalities
\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}
when
{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq 0
and
\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}(\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}})
are motivated by the above reasoning, and allow us to prove subject reduction.
In particular, with
\chi=\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\gamma_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}
we have
\chi\leq{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma_{\mathrm{z}}}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}\leq\mathbf{0}
and
\chi\leq{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\gamma_{\mathrm{s}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}\chi
as desired.
The distributivity and interchange properties specify how
\mathsf{nr}
relates to the other operators.
These are primarily justified by being used in our proof of the substitution lemma (
5.3
) and, consequently, subject reduction (
5.4
).

As a concrete example, consider the erasure modality for which we noted above that
\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\gamma_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}={\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma_{\mathrm{z}}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\gamma_{\mathrm{s}}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}\gamma_{\mathrm{n}}}
is the only lawful definition.
This means that a variable is considered to be erased in
\mathsf{natrec}{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p},{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,z\,s\,n
only if it is erased in all of
z
,
s
, and
n
, or, phrased differently, that all of
z
,
s
, and
n
are considered to be computationally relevant.
This should seem reasonable since, with the value of
n
not being known, both the zero and successor branches may be used at runtime and the natural number argument itself is also relevant simply through matching.
For some other modalities there may be some amount of freedom in how to define
\mathsf{nr}
.
We will discuss this further in
.

The usage judgement is algorithmic in the sense that it induces an algorithm for computing a usage context recursively from the term.
This is possible since all variable-binding subterms have a corresponding annotation giving the newly bound variable its grade.
This observation can be formalized via the following function, which infers a usage context from a term.

Definition 5.2 (Usage inference)
.

The usage inference function
|\_|
, which computes a usage context from a term, is defined recursively as follows:

\displaystyle|\mathsf{U}|

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle|\mathsf{zero}|

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle|\mathbb{N}|

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle|\mathsf{suc}{}\,t|

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|

\displaystyle|\bot|

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle|\mathsf{natrec}{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p},{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,z\,s\,n|

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,|z|\,(\mathsf{tail}\,(\mathsf{tail}\,|s|))\,|n|

\displaystyle|\Pi^{q}_{p}\,F\,G|

\displaystyle\coloneqq|F|+\mathsf{tail}\,|G|

\displaystyle|(t,u)_{\otimes}|

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|+|u|

\displaystyle|\Sigma^{q}\,F\,G|

\displaystyle\coloneqq|F|+\mathsf{tail}\,|G|

\displaystyle|(t,u)_{\&}|

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|\wedge|u|

\displaystyle|\mathsf{x}_{i}|

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{e}_{i}

\displaystyle|\mathsf{fst}{}\,t|

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|

\displaystyle|\lambda^{p}t|

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{tail}\,|t|

\displaystyle|\mathsf{snd}{}\,t|

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|

\displaystyle|t\,^{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}u|

\displaystyle\coloneqq|t|+{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}|u|

\displaystyle|\mathsf{prodrec}{}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,t\,u|

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}r}|t|+\mathsf{tail}\,(\mathsf{tail}\,|u|)

\displaystyle|\mathsf{emptyrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}\,A\,t|

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}|t|

Here
(\mathsf{tail}\,\gamma)(\mathsf{x}_{i})=\gamma(\mathsf{x}_{i+1})
.
Note that
|\_|
makes no checks on the annotations and will produce a context whether a term is well-resourced or not.
However, if the annotations are correct, then the produced context will not only be valid but will also be an upper bound on valid contexts.
In other words, for any well-resourced term, we can always find the most specific context.

Theorem 5.1 (Principality)
.

If
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
for some
\gamma
, then
|t|\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
and
\gamma\leq|t|
.

Proof.

By induction on
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
using the monotonicity of the operators.
∎

Additionally, we get decidability of the usage relation (if one can decide whether
\mathsf{Prodrec}\,{r}
holds for any
r
).

Theorem 5.2 (Decidability)
.

For any usage context
\gamma
and term
t
, both
|t|\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
and
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
are decidable.

Proof.

A lemma showing that either
|t|\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
, or that
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
does not hold for any
\gamma
, can be shown by induction on
t
.
Decidability of
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
can then be proved using decidability of the partial order and
5.1
.
∎

The definition of the usage relation was mainly motivated by a desire to ensure that subject reduction holds, but before we prove it we need to establish a substitution lemma.
The usage relation is designed with the substitution lemma in mind as well, in particular the application,
\mathsf{prodrec}
and
\mathsf{natrec}
cases where substitutions correspond to scaling a context.
When substitutions act on terms they can be considered as maps between terms that may change the number of free variables, or, alternatively, as maps between typing contexts under which the terms are well-formed.
In the same way, usage substitutions correspond to mappings between usage contexts and these maps turn out to be linear
(
DBLP:journals/corr/abs-2005-02247
;
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/AbelB20
)
, in the sense that they can be
represented by matrices
that contain grades.
By treating usage contexts as vectors for which the
i
-th component is the grade associated with
\mathsf{x}_{i}
, substitutions become
matrix multiplications
.

The key to this is, of course, that the matrix must be chosen in such a way that it accurately represents the substitution.
We generalize the usage relation to substitutions, with
\Psi\blacktriangleright{}\sigma
meaning that substitution matrix
\Psi
corresponds to substitution
\sigma
.

Definition 5.3 (Substitution usage)
.

\Psi\blacktriangleright{}\sigma
iff
\forall\mathsf{x}_{i}.\,\mathbf{e}_{i}\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}{\mathsf{x}_{i}[\sigma]}
.

Here
\mathbf{e}_{i}\Psi
is the
i
-th row of
\Psi
.
In other words, a valid substitution matrix should have rows corresponding to valid contexts for each term in the substitution.

The identity substitution corresponds to the identity matrix. A matrix for the single term substitution
(\mathsf{id},u)
—of the kind that occurs during
\beta
-reduction—can be constructed by appending a context matching
u
to the bottom of the identity matrix.
The substitution lemma for grade usage can now be formulated.

Theorem 5.3 (Substitution)
.

If
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
and
\Psi\blacktriangleright{}\sigma
then
\gamma\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}{t[\sigma]}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
.
∎

Note that for
\beta
-reductions, with
\gamma.p\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
and
\delta\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}u
and the matrix constructed as above, we get
\gamma+p\delta\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}{t[u]}
, and
\gamma+p\delta
is the context that is used for applications in the usage relation.
Observations of this kind are key for subject reduction.

Theorem 5.4 (Subject reduction)
.

If
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t
and
\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow{}u\mathrel{:}A
then
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}u
.

Proof.

By induction on
\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow{}u\mathrel{:}A
using
5.3
.
∎

Since valid substitution matrices consist of usage contexts corresponding to substituted terms, context inference can be used to infer a substitution matrix from a substitution.
Formally, we define a substitution inference function
\|\_\|
that computes a substitution matrix from a substitution.

Definition 5.4 (Substitution usage inference)
.

The substitution inference function
\|\_\|
, which infers a substitution matrix from a substitution, is defined such that for a substitution taking terms of
m
free variables to terms of
n
free variables,
\|\sigma\|
is an
m\times n
-matrix such that the
i
-th row is
\big|{\mathsf{x}_{i}[\sigma]}\big|
.

Similarly to the situation for contexts there is no guarantee that
\|\sigma\|
is a valid substitution matrix for
\sigma
, but it is valid if a valid substitution matrix exists.

Theorem 5.5 (Principality)
.

If
\Psi\blacktriangleright{}\sigma
for some
\Psi
, then
\|\sigma\|\blacktriangleright{}\sigma
.

Proof.

By applying
5.1
to every term in
\sigma
and its corresponding row in
\Psi
.
∎

6.
Erasure case study

In order to show our system in practice, we now turn our attention to a specific use case of
\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}
, using a modality for erasure.
The goal is to use annotations to keep track of parts of programs that are not used during evaluation and thus can be safely removed.
We will perform this erasure while compiling programs to an untyped lambda calculus and finally prove the process to be sound in the sense that it does not affect the outcome of evaluating the program.

We could perform this case study using the erasure modality introduced above.
However, the zero can be interpreted as erasure for other modality structures as well, if the zero satisfies certain properties:

Definition 6.1 (Well-behaved zero)
.

A modality structure is said to have a well-behaved zero if the following properties hold:

•

Addition is positive: if
p+q=0
, then
p=0
and
q=0
.

•

Meet is positive: if
p\wedge q=0
then
p=0
and
q=0
.

•

The
\mathsf{nr}
function is positive in its last three arguments: if
\mathsf{nr}_{p}^{r}\,q_{z}\,q_{s}\,q_{n}=0
then
q_{z}=0
,
q_{s}=0
, and
q_{n}=0
.

•

The zero-product property holds: if
pq=0
then
p=0
or
q=0
.

•

The multiplicative zero and unit are distinct, that is,
0\neq 1
.

The first property is discussed by
DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
and the fourth one by
DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18
.

DBLP:conf/birthday/McBride16
also discusses the requirement that
0
should be maximal:
11
11

11

Since McBride flips the order on grades, our maximal means minimal in their setting.

this
follows
from the second property.

For the remainder of the section, we will work with an arbitrary modality structure with a well-behaved zero.
We expect that any quantitative modality structure has a well-behaved zero.
In particular, this is the case for the structures for
erasure
,
linear types
,
affine types
and
linear or affine types
introduced above, but
not for the unit modality
.
It would also fail for e.g.modality structures for timing
(
ghicaSmith:esop14
, Section 3)
where negative grades would allow you to “borrow” time from the future.

Though the modality structure may contain any number of elements, for erasure we only need to keep track of whether during evaluation a variable is used or not.
The annotation
0
will be used to represent the latter and any other annotation will be interpreted as the former.
For convenience, we will borrow the notation of the erasure modality structure and denote any non-zero grade as
\omega
.
Through subsumption, noting that
0\wedge 1<0
,
variables that are not used may be labelled as either used or not,
giving the programmer a choice of whether they want them to be erased or not.

We first show that our intended interpretation seems reasonable by the following lemma.

Theorem 6.1 (Variable usage)
.

If
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{x}_{i}
then
\gamma(\mathsf{x}_{i})\neq 0
.

Proof.

From
\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{x}_{i}
we can conclude that
\gamma\leq\mathbf{e}_{i}
, and in particular
\gamma(\mathsf{x}_{i})\leq 1
, but for a well-behaved zero
0\not\leq 1
.
∎

In short, the lemma states that when the usage relation checks a variable its grade must not be
0
.
This does not necessarily mean, however, that all occurring variables are computationally relevant.
As an example of this, consider the
polymorphic identity function

\mathsf{id}\coloneq\lambda^{0}A.\lambda^{\omega}x.x
, written here with named variables for convenience, where the type argument
A
is erased (using the two-element erasure instance for concreteness).
This term has
type

\Pi^{p}_{0}\,(A:\mathsf{U})\,(\Pi^{q}_{\omega}\,(x:A)\,A)
and is
well-resourced
with respect to the zero context.
In the context
B\mathrel{:}\mathsf{U}.b\mathrel{:}B
the
term

(\lambda^{0}A.\lambda^{\omega}x.x)\,^{0}B\,^{\omega}b
has
type

B
.
Since the type argument
B
is not used during evaluation it should be erasable and indeed, this term is
well-resourced
with respect to the context
B\mathrel{:}0.b\mathrel{:}\omega
(where
b
is marked as relevant and
B
as erasable):

\frac{\frac{\frac{}{B\mathrel{:}0.b\mathrel{:}0\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\lambda^{0}A.\lambda^{\omega}x.x}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{}{B\mathrel{:}\omega.b\mathrel{:}0\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}B}}{B\mathrel{:}0.b\mathrel{:}0\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}(\lambda^{0}A.\lambda^{\omega}x.x)\,^{0}B}\quad\frac{}{B\mathrel{:}0.b\mathrel{:}\omega\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}b}}{B\mathrel{:}0.b\mathrel{:}\omega\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}(\lambda^{0}A.\lambda^{\omega}x.x)\,^{0}B\,^{\omega}b}

The key is that for erasable applications, any context can be used to check the function argument since it will anyway be scaled by
0
.
Erasable variables are thus allowed to occur in places such as erasable function arguments.

Since we are now able to track whether a term is computationally relevant or not, we can move on to the task of erasing unneeded parts.
As already mentioned, we do this by compiling
\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}
to an untyped lambda calculus.
The syntax of this target language is the following:

\displaystyle\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Target.html#Term\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{i}\;|\;\lambda\,t\;|\;t\,u\;|\;(t,u)\;|\;\mathsf{fst}{}\,t\;|\;\mathsf{snd}{}\,t\;|\;\mathsf{prodrec}{}\,t\,u\;|\;\mathsf{zero}\;|\;\mathsf{suc}{}\,t\;|\;\mathsf{natrec}{}\,t\,u\,v\;|\;\mathord{\lightning}

This language is, except for the lack of types and grades, very similar to
\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}
and we re-use the notation for substitutions.
Notably however, the target language also includes the term
\mathord{\lightning}
, which represents an undefined value.
We support two operational semantics for the target language as shown in
fig.

6
, one
non-strict
(call-by-name) in the same style as for
\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}
, and one
strict
(call-by-value).
These differ only in evaluation of applications.
For the strict semantics, the argument must first be reduced to a value before
\beta
-reduction can trigger.
In
fig.

6
we have marked rules and premises which only apply to the strict semantics with a
red
background and we will do so also in the following, similarly marking parts that apply only in the non-strict case with a
green
background.

\frac{\hbox to37.29pt{\vbox to11.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\frac{v\longrightarrow{}v^{\prime}}{\mathsf{fst}{}\,v\longrightarrow{}\mathsf{fst}{}\,v^{\prime}}\frac{v\longrightarrow{}v^{\prime}}{\mathsf{snd}{}\,v\longrightarrow{}\mathsf{snd}{}\,v^{\prime}}\frac{}{\mathsf{fst}{}\,(v,w)\longrightarrow{}v}\frac{}{\mathsf{snd}{}\,(v,w)\longrightarrow{}w}\frac{v\longrightarrow{}v^{\prime}}{\mathsf{prodrec}{}\,v\,w\longrightarrow{}\mathsf{prodrec}{}\,v^{\prime}\,w}\frac{}{\mathsf{prodrec}{}\,(v,v^{\prime})\,w\longrightarrow{}{w[v,v^{\prime}]}}\frac{v\longrightarrow{}v^{\prime}}{\mathsf{natrec}{}\,z\,s\,v\longrightarrow{}\mathsf{natrec}{}\,z\,s\,v^{\prime}}\frac{}{\mathsf{natrec}{}\,z\,s\,\mathsf{zero}\longrightarrow{}z}\frac{}{\mathsf{natrec}{}\,z\,s\,(\mathsf{suc}{}\,v)\longrightarrow{}{s[v,\mathsf{natrec}{}\,z\,s\,v]}}\frac{}{v\longrightarrow^{*}v}\frac{v\longrightarrow{}v^{\prime}\quad v^{\prime}\longrightarrow^{*}w}{v\longrightarrow^{*}w}

\Description

Weak head reduction of target language terms,
v\longrightarrow{}w
and its reflexive, transitive closure
v\longrightarrow^{*}w
. Premises and rules with a gray background apply only to the strict semantics.

Figure 6.
Weak head reduction of target language terms,
v\longrightarrow{}w
and its reflexive, transitive closure
v\longrightarrow^{*}w
. Premises and rules with a gray background apply only to the strict semantics.

The compiler is implemented as a function
\_^{\bullet}
, taking
\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}
terms to terms of the target language.

\end{displaymath}
\Description{Extraction/erasure function. Here $\omega$ stands for any grade distinct from $0$.}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{7}}{\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Extraction.html#erase. Here $\omega$ stands for any
grade distinct from $0$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{Figure 7}}{\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Extraction.html#erase. Here $\omega$ stands for any
grade distinct from $0$.}}}
\end{figure}
\par Because the structure of the languages is similar, the definition is mostly straightforward, following the structure of the terms.
For erased lambdas and applications as well as types the extraction functions differ depending on if we target the strict or non-strict semantics.
\par Let us first look at the non-strict case where there are a couple of things to note.
Firstly, most erasable code is extracted to \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Target.Non-terminating.html#loop, which is the \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Target.Non-terminating.html#loop-reduces-forever $(\lambda\,\mathsf{x}_{0}\,\mathsf{x}_{0})\,(\lambda\,\mathsf{x}_{0}\,\mathsf{x}_{0})$, or $(\lambda x.x\,x)\,(\lambda x.x\,x)$ with named variables.
The idea is that terms which we extract to $\mathord{\circlearrowleft}$ should be unused during evaluation, so introducing such loops should not have any bad consequences.
By the end of this section, we will have shown that this is indeed the case.
Any type, as well as $\mathsf{emptyrec}$ are extracted directly to $\mathord{\circlearrowleft}$ as these should never be evaluated and have no proper representation in the target language.
\par Secondly, for lambdas, applications, and $\mathsf{prodrec}$ we make a case distinction on the grade annotation of the argument or scrutinee, respectively.
When it is non-zero, the extraction procedure keeps the structure of the term and extracts the sub-terms as usual.
When the grade is zero, we have encountered an erasable part of the program which is removed by the extraction function.
Erasable function arguments are simply discarded, keeping only the function.
Correspondingly, lambdas taking erasable function arguments are extracted by dropping the lambda and substituting in $\mathord{\circlearrowleft}$ for the erased argument.
Similarly, for erased uses of $\mathsf{prodrec}$, the scrutinee is discarded and $\mathord{\circlearrowleft}$ is substituted in for the components.
As an example, consider \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Examples.html#id-%E2%84%95-zero, the identity function applied to $\mathbb{N}$ and $\mathsf{zero}$.
This (closed) term has \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Examples.html#%E2%8A%A2id-%E2%84%95-zero $\mathbb{N}$ and is \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Examples.html#%E2%96%B8id-%E2%84%95-zero.
As we saw above, the type argument is erasable and indeed, both it and the corresponding lambda are removed and we have \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Examples.html#erase-non-strict-id-%E2%84%95-zero.
\par For the most part, extraction to the strict target language is the same but there are a few notable differences.
Since functions are now strict we now keep lambdas and erased function arguments are replaced with the dummy value $\mathord{\lightning}$.
Similarly, types are now extracted to $\mathord{\lightning}$ instead of $\mathord{\circlearrowleft}$.
Note that $\mathord{\circlearrowleft}$ is still used in the extraction of $\mathsf{prodrec}$ and $\mathsf{emptyrec}$.
In the former case, the components of an erased pair will not be evaluated as before and the latter case still represents an impossible case that will not be evaluated.
In this case, the example above is extracted differently, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Examples.html#erase-strict-id-ℕ-zero, where both lambdas are kept but the type argument is replaced with $\mathord{\lightning}$.
\par An alternative would have been to keep lambdas and $\mathsf{prodrec}$ in both the strict and non-strict cases and instead replaced their arguments by $\mathord{\circlearrowleft}$ and $(\mathord{\circlearrowleft},\mathord{\circlearrowleft})$ respectively.
Such an approach is also feasible as we have shown in our original article \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{icfp-version}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} where we did take such an approach for the non-strict target language.
Conversely, it would \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Consequences.Soundness.html#no-run-time-canonicity-if-strict-and-arguments-removed to remove lambdas in the strict case since one could then cause a problematic (e.g.looping or crashing) term to be evaluated.
\par If correctly designed, the extraction function should satisfy two main properties: (1) that it erases everything erasable and (2) that it is sound in the sense that it does not affect the outcome of evaluating the program.
The first property is straightforward to prove.
\par\inLinkedTheorem[Erasure is complete]If $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t$ and $\gamma(\mathsf{x}_{i})=0$ then $\mathsf{x}_{i}$ does not occur in $t^{\bullet}$.
\endinLinkedTheorem
\@proof By induction on $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t$ using \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:eraseVar}.
\qed\end@proof
\par For the example we can see that extraction is sound: the term reduces to $\mathsf{zero}$ both \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Examples.html#id-%E2%84%95-zero%E2%87%92%2Azero and \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Examples.html#erase-id-%E2%84%95-zero%E2%87%92%2Azero extraction.
In the remainder of this section we shall demonstrate that the extraction function is sound in general,
meaning that programs retain their value under erasure.
\par We could restrict the term \emph{programs} to closed expressions of type $\mathbb{N}$.
However, in practical dependently typed programming it can be convenient to work under extra assumptions
that are not computationally relevant but can be used to establish correctness properties.
For example, one might want to use the law of excluded middle, a choice principle, some other extension
of type theory, or simply a correctness conjecture that one intends to prove later.
The erasure grades allow us to track which assumptions are not computationally relevant as they only appear in erased parts.
Thus, we aim at correct erasure for programs $\Delta_{0}\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}$ with $\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t$,
meaning that assumptions from $\Delta_{0}$ appear in $t$ only in erased positions.
This situation is reminiscent of that of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{negativeAxioms}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} who show that one retain canonicity when all types in $\Delta_{0}$ are ``negative'', meaning that they are either the empty type or functions with a negative type as co-domain.
Similarly to erased ones, such assumptions have no computational content and can thus not be used to produce, e.g., natural numbers.
\par However, in order to support open programs we have to be careful so that $t$ does not get stuck through a match, in a non-erased position, on an erased term.
In our case, there are two sources of such \emph{erased matches}:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Eliminating the empty type with $\mathsf{emptyrec}_{0}$.
We can exclude this either by requiring the side condition $\mathsf{Emptyrec}\,{0}$ in the usage relation to be false unless $\Delta_{0}$ is empty, or by requiring our assumptions to be consistent, i.e., there should be no $u$ such that $\Delta_{0}\vdash{}u\mathrel{:}\bot$.\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Eliminating an erased weak pair with $\mathsf{prodrec}{}_{0}^{q}$:
if applied to a neutral pair, $\mathsf{prodrec}$ does not reduce.
Thus, we disallow matching on erased weak pairs in non-erased positions by requiring the side condition $\mathsf{Prodrec}\,{0}$ to be false unless $\Delta_{0}$ is empty---in which case there are no neutral terms to get stuck on.
\end{enumerate}
Some further discussion can be found in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:erasedMatching}.
\par Our soundness proof uses a logical relation for erasure
which we will simply call \emph{the logical relation}.
The logical relation relates a term $t$ in the source language with a term $v$ in the target language.
It might seem natural to define the relation by induction on the type of $t$, but dependent types do not directly give us a suitable induction principle.
Instead, we first define another logical relation, \emph{the logical relation for types}, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.LogicalRelation.Simplified.html#_%E2%8A%A8_ which provides us with such a principle.
\par\inLinkedDefinition[Logical relation for types]Given a context $\Gamma$, the logical relation for types is expressed by the relation
$\Gamma\vDash A$, defined inductively in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:typeCode}.
Herein, we write $\mathscr{A}::J$ to indicate that $\mathscr{A}$ is a proof of judgement $J$.
\endinLinkedDefinition\begin{figure}\begin{equation*}\frac{\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}\mathsf{U}}{\Gamma\vDash A}\quad\textsc{left={$\langle\mathsf{U}\rangle$},vcenter}\hskip-10.0pt\frac{\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}\mathbb{N}}{\Gamma\vDash A}\quad\textsc{left={$\langle\mathbb{N}\rangle$},vcenter}\hskip-10.0pt\frac{\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}\bot}{\Gamma\vDash A}\quad\textsc{left={$\langle\bot\rangle$},vcenter}\hskip-10.0pt\frac{\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}K\quad\mathsf{Ne}\,K}{\Gamma\vDash A}\quad\textsc{left={$\langle\mathsf{Ne}\,K\rangle$},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}\Pi^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}\,F\,G\quad\mathscr{F}::\Gamma\vDash F\quad\mathscr{G}::(\forall a.\ \Gamma\vdash{}a\mathrel{:}F\Rightarrow\Gamma\vDash{G[a]})}{\Gamma\vDash A}\quad\textsc{left={$\langle\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}FG/\mathscr{F}/\mathscr{G}\rangle$},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}\Sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}\,F\,G\quad\mathscr{F}::\Gamma\vDash F\quad\mathscr{G}::(\forall a.\ \Gamma\vdash{}a\mathrel{:}F\Rightarrow\Gamma\vDash{G[a]})}{\Gamma\vDash A}\quad\textsc{left={$\langle\Sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}FG/\mathscr{F}/\mathscr{G}\rangle$},vcenter}\end{equation*}\Description{The logical relation for types, $\Gamma\vDash A$.}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{8}}{The logical relation for types, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.LogicalRelation.Simplified.html#_%E2%8A%A8_.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{Figure 8}}{The logical relation for types, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.LogicalRelation.Simplified.html#_%E2%8A%A8_.}}}
\end{figure}
\par Roughly speaking, $A$ is in the relation if it reduces to a canonical type with subexpressions in the relation, or if it reduces to a neutral type.
Type families $G$ are in the relation if all of their instantiations $G[a]$ with well-typed terms $a$ are in the relation;
this gives us the required induction principle for $\Pi$- and $\Sigma$-types.
\par The relation is defined inductively with one case for each type former.
We will use labels such as $\langle\mathbb{N}\rangle$
and $\langle\Pi_{p}^{q}FG/\mathscr{F}/\mathscr{G}\rangle$
to indicate the different cases.
In the latter case, $A$ reduces to $\Pi^{q}_{p}\,F\,G$, derivation $\mathscr{F}$ is a proof that $F$ is in the relation and $\mathscr{G}(a)$ is a proof that ${G[a]}$ is in the relation for any well-typed term $a$ of type $F$.
Note that when we write $\mathscr{G}(a)$, we leave the proof that $a$ is well-typed implicit.
\par The logical relation for types is based on (a modified version of) the logical relation for reducibility used by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} but has fewer assumptions making it easier to work with.
In comparison, the reducibility relation of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} requires the domain and co-domain of $\Pi$-types (and similar for $\Sigma$-types) to be reducible under arbitrary weakenings.
Without such assumptions, we cannot prove the fundamental lemma for the types relation directly, but we obtain it from the fundamental lemma of the reducibility relation.
\par\inLinkedTheorem[Type reducibility]If\/ $\Gamma\vdash{}A$ then $\Gamma\vDash A$.
\endinLinkedTheorem
\@proof By the fundamental lemma for reducibility, $A$ is in the reducibility relation. It follows that $\Gamma\vDash A$.
\qed\end@proof
\par We will now define the erasure relation $t\mathrel{\circledR}v\mathrel{:}A\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$ in which we relate a source language term $t$ of type $A$ to a target language term $v$.
The relation is defined such that $t$ and $v$ ``behave the same'' when evaluated and the intent is to show that $t$ is related to $t^{\bullet}$ (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:erasedFundamental}).
We define the relation by recursion on $\mathscr{A}$, a proof that $A$ is in the logical relation for types, essentially allowing us to define it by cases on the type.
As above, there are some differences depending on whether we target the strict or non-strict semantics.
Notably, we add some additional requirements related to strictness when targeting the strict semantics and handle erased functions differently to account for them being extracted differently.
\par For the remainder of this section, we fix a consistent context $\Delta_{0}$ with $\vdash{}\Delta_{0}$.
\par\inLinkedDefinition[Erasure logical relation]The logical relation for erasure, $t\mathrel{\circledR}v\mathrel{:}A\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$, where $t$ and $A$ are $\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}$ terms and $v$ is a target language term, is defined by recursion on $\mathscr{A}::\Delta_{0}\vDash A$ as follows:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@If $\mathscr{A}=\langle\mathsf{U}\rangle$ then $t\mathrel{\circledR}v\mathrel{:}A\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$ holds iff \hbox to32.58pt{\vbox to14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{}}{}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{16.29047pt}{7.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{}}{}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{47.53818pt}{7.25891pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 20.09 L 131.56 20.09 L 131.56 0 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5898,0.2734,0.2734}\lxSVG@setdash{0.5pt,1.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.35 0.35 L 0.35 19.74 L 131.21 19.74 L 131.21 0.35 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.5pt}{4.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{59}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\end{itemize}\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@If $\mathscr{A}=\langle\Sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}FG/\mathscr{F}/\mathscr{G}\rangle$ then $t\mathrel{\circledR}v\mathrel{:}A\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$ iff there are terms $t_{1},t_{2},v_{1},v_{2}$ such that
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\Delta_{0}\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{*}(t_{1},t_{2})_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}\mathrel{:}\Sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}\,F\,G$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$v\longrightarrow^{*}(v_{1},v_{2})$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\Delta_{0}\vdash{}t_{1}\mathrel{:}F$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$t_{1}\mathrel{\circledR}v_{1}\mathrel{:}F\mathbin{/}\mathscr{F}$, and
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$t_{2}\mathrel{\circledR}v_{2}\mathrel{:}{G[t_{1}]}\mathbin{/}\mathscr{G}(t_{1})$.
\end{itemize}\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@If $\mathscr{A}=\langle\bot\rangle$ or $\mathscr{A}=\langle\mathsf{Ne}\,K\rangle$ then $t\mathrel{\circledR}v\mathrel{:}A\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$ does not hold.
\end{itemize}\endinLinkedDefinition
\par For base types, the idea is that source and target term should represent the same value of the right type.
In the universe case we put no requirements on neither $t$ nor $v$ when targeting the non-strict semantics and only require $v$ to reduce to $\mathord{\lightning}$ in the strict case.
This is done since $t$ is then a type, and there are no types in the target language.
The natural number case is defined recursively.
Either both terms reduce to $\mathsf{zero}$, or to the successor of some terms which in turn need to be related, and the target term must be a numeral in the strict case.
\par$\Pi^{q}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}$-types are treated differently depending on whether the function argument is erasable or not.
Non-erasable applications (${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}=\omega$) need only be related when the arguments are related.
For erasable functions (${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}=0$) the function arguments do not matter.
In the strict case, we thus use $\mathord{\lightning}$ as the argument on the target language side, without requiring it to be related to the source language argument, while in the non-strict case there is no argument at all.
This matches the application cases of the extraction function.
For both erased and non-erased functions we also require the target language term to evaluate to a lambda in the strict case.
\par$\Sigma$-types are fairly similar to the base types and no difference is made between weak and strong variants:
related terms should reduce to pairs whose components are pairwise related.
Finally, for the empty type and neutral types, no terms are in the logical relation.
The reason for the former is that the consistency of $\Delta_{0}$ ensures that there are no terms of type $\bot$.
Neutral types are excluded as part of a simplification of only considering non-neutral terms.
A consequence of this simplification is that we can only prove the fundamental lemma for empty contexts $\Delta_{0}$ or when erased matches are disallowed for weak $\Sigma$-types (see also \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:erasedMatching}).
\par In order to prove the fundamental lemma we generalize the relation above so that it holds under substitutions to $\Delta_{0}$.
Let us first define well-typed substitutions.
Intuitively, a substitution $\sigma$ is well-typed, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.Substitution.Primitive.Primitive.html#_%E2%8A%A2%CB%A2_%E2%88%B7_, if all its entries are well-typed terms.
The formal definition is given in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:subst-typing}.
\par\begin{figure}\begin{equation*}\frac{}{\Delta\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\epsilon}\frac{\Delta\vdash\mathsf{tail}\,\sigma\mathrel{:}\Gamma\quad\Delta\vdash{}\mathsf{head}\,\sigma\mathrel{:}{A[\mathsf{tail}\,\sigma]}}{\Delta\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Gamma.A}\end{equation*}\Description{Well-typed substitutions $\Delta\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Gamma$}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{9}}{Well-typed substitutions \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.Substitution.Primitive.Primitive.html#_%E2%8A%A2%CB%A2_%E2%88%B7_}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{Figure 9}}{Well-typed substitutions \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.Substitution.Primitive.Primitive.html#_%E2%8A%A2%CB%A2_%E2%88%B7_}}}
\end{figure}
\par We then define the logical relation for substitutions, relating substitutions from the source and target languages.
\par\inLinkedDefinition[Substitution logical relation]The logical relation for substitutions,
$\sigma\mathrel{\circledR}\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleleft$}}\gamma$,
relates substitutions $\sigma$ and $\sigma^{\prime}$ from $\lambda^{\Sigma\Pi\mathrm{U}\mathbb{N}}_{\mathcal{M}}$ and the target language, respectively,
under contexts $\Gamma$ and $\gamma$.
It is defined by recursion on the contexts $\Gamma$ and $\gamma$ (which have the same length).
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@If $\Gamma=\epsilon$ and $\gamma=\epsilon$ then $\sigma\mathrel{\circledR}\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleleft$}}\gamma$ holds unconditionally.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@If $\Gamma=\Delta.A$ and $\gamma=\delta.{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}$ then $\sigma\mathrel{\circledR}\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleleft$}}\gamma$ iff $\mathsf{tail}\,\sigma\mathrel{\circledR}\mathsf{tail}\,\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleleft$}}\delta$ and if ${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}\neq 0$ then $\mathsf{head}\,\sigma\mathrel{\circledR}\mathsf{head}\,\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{:}{A[\mathsf{tail}\,\sigma]}\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$ for some $\mathscr{A}::\Delta_{0}\vDash{A[\mathsf{tail}\,\sigma]}$.
\end{itemize}\endinLinkedDefinition
\par The idea is to think of substitutions as lists of terms, one for each free variable.
These are required to be pairwise related, except for terms corresponding to erasable variables.
The motivation for this is related to \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:eraseVar} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{thm:erasedVar}:
since erasable variables will not occur in non-erasable settings,
and not at all in the target language term,
one should be able to substitute anything (of the correct type) for them without consequence.
\par The final piece of the logical relation is erasure validity, which both expresses the closure of the logical relation under substitutions and our goal that terms should be related to their extracted counterparts.
\inLinkedDefinition[Validity]The validity relation for erasure,
$\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\Gamma\Vdash t\mathrel{:}A$,
is defined to hold iff for all substitutions $\sigma$, $\sigma^{\prime}$
such that $\Delta_{0}\vdash\sigma\mathrel{:}\Gamma$
and $\sigma\mathrel{\circledR}\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleleft$}}\gamma$
we have ${t[\sigma]}\mathrel{\circledR}{t^{\bullet}[\sigma^{\prime}]}\mathrel{:}{A[\sigma]}\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$
for some $\mathscr{A}::\Delta_{0}\vDash{A[\sigma]}$.
\endinLinkedDefinition
\par Before proving the fundamental lemma we prove a couple of crucial properties.
The first expresses that related terms should correspond to the same value in the sense that they reduce to the same weak head normal form.
In other words, we may replace a term with any other term in the same reduction chain without leaving the relation.
\par\inLinkedTheorem[Closure under expansion]If $\mathscr{A}::\Gamma\vDash A$
and $\Delta_{0}\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{*}t^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A$ and $v\longrightarrow^{*}v^{\prime}$ and $t^{\prime}\mathrel{\circledR}v^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$ then $t\mathrel{\circledR}v\mathrel{:}A\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$.
\endinLinkedTheorem
\@proof By induction on $t^{\prime}\mathrel{\circledR}v^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par The second property can be seen as a different form of the subsumption rule in the usage relation, allowing us to change the quantity or grade context in the substitution and validity relations.
\inLinkedTheorem[Subsumption]If $\gamma(\mathsf{x}_{i})=0$ implies $\delta(\mathsf{x}_{i})=0$ for all $\mathsf{x}_{i}$, then
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@if $\sigma\mathrel{\circledR}\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleleft$}}\gamma$, then $\sigma\mathrel{\circledR}\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleleft$}}\delta$, and
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@if $\delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\Gamma\Vdash t\mathrel{:}A$ then $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\Gamma\Vdash t\mathrel{:}A$.
\end{itemize}\endinLinkedTheorem
\@proof For substitutions, by induction on $\Gamma$ and $\gamma$.
The validity case then follows.
\qed\end@proof
This property is important for our proof of the fundamental lemma since it allows us to re-fit a proof of related substitutions to the correct usage context for each subterm.
\par\inLinkedTheorem[Fundamental lemma]If\/ $\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}A$ and $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t$, then $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\Gamma\Vdash t\mathrel{:}A$.
\endinLinkedTheorem
\@proof By induction on $\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}A$ using \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:LRred}, \lx@cref{refnum}{thm:subsumption} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{thm:eraseVar}.
\qed\end@proof
\par The fundamental lemma implies that for any related substitutions $\sigma$ and $\sigma^{\prime}$, ${t[\sigma]}$ is related to ${t^{\bullet}[\sigma^{\prime}]}$.
We would like to apply this result to identity substitutions, but naturally first need to show that these are related.
More generally, \emph{all} substitutions \emph{from erasable contexts} are related.
\par\inLinkedTheorem[Erased substitutions]$\sigma\mathrel{\circledR}\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\blacktriangleleft$}}\mathbf{0}$
for all $\sigma$ and $\sigma^{\prime}$.
\endinLinkedTheorem
\par\inLinkedTheorem[Logical erasure soundness]If $\Delta_{0}\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}A$ and $\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t$ then there is $\mathscr{A}::\Delta_{0}\vDash A$ such that $t\mathrel{\circledR}t^{\bullet}\mathrel{:}A\mathbin{/}\mathscr{A}$.
\endinLinkedTheorem
\@proof By the fundamental lemma applied to identity substitutions and \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:erasedSubst}.
\qed\end@proof
\par With the fundamental lemma proven, we can finally move on to showing that the extraction function is sound for programs computing natural numbers.
In the source language, terms only reduce to weak head normal form so the final result of evaluating such a program will either be $\mathsf{zero}$ or $\mathsf{suc}{}\,t$ for some $t$ which might not be in WHNF.
The same is true for the target language when the non-strict semantics are used (in the strict case the logical relation requires that $t$ is a numeral).
In other words, reducing a term to WHNF is not enough to determine whether two terms in WHNF represent the same number or not.
To circumvent this we extend the reduction relations of both the source and the non-strict target languages to allow reduction under $\mathsf{suc}$, see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:sucred},
with reflexive-transitive closures $\longrightarrow^{s*}$ as expected.
\begin{figure}\begin{equation*}\frac{\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow{}t^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}}{\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{s}t^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{s}t^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}}{\Gamma\vdash{}\mathsf{suc}{}\,t\longrightarrow^{s}\mathsf{suc}{}\,t^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}}\frac{v\longrightarrow{}v^{\prime}}{v\longrightarrow^{s}v^{\prime}}\hbox to73.33pt{\vbox to12.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{}}{}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{36.66344pt}{6.0978pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{}}{}
