\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10}}{Extended reduction relations, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.SucRed.html#_%E2%8A%A2_%E2%87%92%CB%A2_%E2%88%B7%E2%84%95 and \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.SucRed.html#_%E2%87%92%CB%A2_.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{Figure 10}}{Extended reduction relations, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.SucRed.html#_%E2%8A%A2_%E2%87%92%CB%A2_%E2%88%B7%E2%84%95 and \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.SucRed.html#_%E2%87%92%CB%A2_.}}}
\end{figure}
\par Writing $\underline{n}$ for the numeral $\mathsf{suc}^{n}\;\mathsf{zero}$, where $\mathsf{suc}^{0}\;t$ is $t$ and $\mathsf{suc}^{k+1}\;t$ is $\mathsf{suc}{}\,(\mathsf{suc}^{k}\;t)$, we give the soundness theorem.
\inLinkedTheorem[Operational erasure soundness]Let $\Delta$ be a context.
If\/ $\mathsf{Prodrec}\,{0}$ is false or $\Delta$ is empty,
$\Delta$ is consistent if $\mathsf{Emptyrec}\,{0}$ is true,
and furthermore
$\Delta\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}$ and $\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t$, then
there is a natural number $n$ such that
$\Delta\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{s*}\underline{n}\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}$ and $t^{\bullet}\longrightarrow^{s*}\underline{n}$.
\endinLinkedTheorem
\@proof By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:erasedFundamental} we have $t\mathrel{\circledR}t^{\bullet}\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}\mathbin{/}\langle\mathbb{N}\rangle$.
The proof proceeds by induction on this derivation.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Discussion}
In total the formalization, including the erasure case study, the extensions discussed in \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:unit} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{sec:modes}, and various other additions,
consists of roughly 110000 lines ($5500\text{\,}\mathrm{kB}$)
of Agda code, comments and empty lines not included.
This can be compared to the roughly 13000 lines of code ($700\text{\,}\mathrm{kB}$)
forked from an extension of the development of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
However, the current formalization contains substantial extensions not discussed in this paper, such as identity types, universe polymorphism, further modality structures, and a resource-aware abstract machine to verify quantitative typing beyond erasure.
These extensions contribute roughly half of total lines of code.
\par While some time was spent modifying the Agda development, we believe that extending a pre-existing formalization overall provided great benefit, as it simplified the development process in several ways.
For instance, we did not have to define the language from scratch and the code already included proofs of many non-trivial properties.
We also found the reducibility relation useful as the starting point for defining the logical relation in the erasure case study.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Usage counting for the natural number eliminator}
\par Although the erasure modality structure only has \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Erasure.Properties.html#nr-unique for its $\mathsf{nr}${} function, this is not the case in general.
This leads to some care being required to ensure that one achieves the intended interpretation of the modality.
Taking the linear types structure as an example, one can define \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Zero-one-many.html#zero-one-many-greatest-star-nr which fails to encode linearity.
The issue can be seen by considering a definition of \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Untyped.Nat.html#plus%E2%80%B2 for natural numbers,
$\mathsf{natrec}{}_{0,1}^{0}\,(m.\mathbb{N})\,k\,(m.r.\mathsf{suc}{}\,r)\,n$,
adding variables $k$ and $n$.
It corresponds to the following Agda code which would appear to be linear in both its arguments---corresponding definitions are accepted as linear by both Idris\penalty\ 2 and Linear Haskell:
\par{\par\vskip 12.0pt plus 3.0pt minus 9.0pt\noindent}\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\immediate\ignorespaces\immediate\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\hbox to0.0pt{{\@classiv\@classz\@classiv}\@preamble}\hbox to195.44485pt{\@preamble\ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}\scalebox{0.85}{{plus}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\scalebox{0.85}{{:}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}\scalebox{0.85}{{ℕ}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\scalebox{0.85}{{\textrightarrow}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}\scalebox{0.85}{{ℕ}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\scalebox{0.85}{{\textrightarrow}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}\scalebox{0.85}{{ℕ}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\hbox to0.0pt{{\@classiv\@classz\@classiv}\@preamble}\hbox to289.32237pt{\@preamble\ignorespaces}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\\
}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\hbox to0.0pt{{\@classiv\@classz\@classiv}\@preamble}\hbox to132.4446pt{\@preamble\ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}\scalebox{0.85}{{plus}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{k}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5469,0}\scalebox{0.85}{{zero}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\hbox to0.0pt{{\@classiv\@classz\@classiv}\@preamble}\hbox to158.00023pt{\@preamble\ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\scalebox{0.85}{{=}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{k}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\hbox to0.0pt{{\@classiv\@classz\@classiv}\@preamble}\hbox to194.3224pt{\@preamble\ignorespaces}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\\
}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\hbox to0.0pt{{\@classiv\@classz\@classiv}\@preamble}\hbox to132.4446pt{\@preamble\ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}\scalebox{0.85}{{plus}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{k}} {}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\scalebox{0.85}{{(}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5469,0}\scalebox{0.85}{{suc}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\scalebox{0.85}{{)}}}\mbox{ }}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\hbox to0.0pt{{\@classiv\@classz\@classiv}\@preamble}\hbox to158.00023pt{\@preamble\ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\scalebox{0.85}{{=}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5469,0}\scalebox{0.85}{{suc}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\scalebox{0.85}{{(}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}\scalebox{0.85}{{plus}}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{k}} {}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\scalebox{0.85}{{)}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\@classz\@acol}\hbox to0.0pt{{\@classiv\@classz\@classiv}\@preamble}\hbox to194.3224pt{\@preamble\ignorespaces}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\par\vskip 12.0pt plus 3.0pt minus 9.0pt\noindent}
However, with the $\mathsf{nr}${} function linked above, both arguments are instead \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Linearity.Examples.Bad.Nr.html#%E2%96%B8plus%E2%80%B2-x%E2%82%80-x%E2%82%81, representing a non-linear usage.
Worse, when adding a variable to itself, the variable would be considered to be \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Linearity.Examples.Bad.Nr.html#%E2%96%B8plus%E2%80%B2-x%E2%82%80-x%E2%82%80 (assigned grade $1$) even though it is used twice.
\par The $\mathsf{nr}${} function described here matches the ``natrec-star'' operator for linear types that we discuss in our original article \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{icfp-version}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
While there are other ways to define the operator for the linear types modality, none would be able to solve both issues which is why we have
here replaced it with the more general $\mathsf{nr}${} functions.
This approach makes it possible to avoid these kinds of issues by choosing an appropriate function.
However, as noted above it is still possible to define ``bad'' functions so there should perhaps be further properties of $\mathsf{nr}${} functions in order to ensure correct grade assignment.
However, since our main goal here is correctness of erasure, and our case study shows that the constraints we impose on $\mathsf{nr}${} functions are sufficient to ensure this, we have not explored this matter.
Elsewhere \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{types25Natrec}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, we have considered an alternative method where the usage context assigned to $\mathsf{natrec}$ is not computed but instead defined in terms of certain lower bounds (which are related to the characteristic inequalities we require for $\mathsf{nr}${} functions), but this method is not in general compatible with our usage inference procedure.
There have also been various different approaches to handling inductive data types (like natural numbers) in the context of QTT \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{qttPolyFunctor,quantitativeInductive,polyTime}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
Unlike our approach, these have the benefit of not putting the responsibility of assigning correct usage contexts on the programmer (or whoever defines the $\mathsf{nr}${} function).
Another possible approach could be to make the assigned grade context depend on the natural number argument \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{types25Linear}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
This feature is available to some extent in the Granule language \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{DBLP:journals/pacmpl/OrchardLE19}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} allowing for a more accurate usage counting in certain situations.
\par As noted above, one should be careful when defining $\mathsf{nr}${} functions as to ensure that they satisfy the intended interpretation of the modalities.
Although a correctness proof beyond erasure is out of scope of this work, we will now define $\mathsf{nr}${} functions for our example instances that we believe to be suitable.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{Affine Types.}\ Recall from the usage rule for $\mathsf{natrec}$ (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:usage}) that in $\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}$, the grades $p$ and $r$ correspond to the uses of the predecessor and recursive call in the successor case while ${\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}$, ${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}$, and ${\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}$ correspond to the uses of the zero branch, the successor branch, and the natural number, respectively.
\par If ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=0$, then the recursive call is unused.
Essentially, this corresponds to $\mathsf{natrec}$ being used only to match on the natural number argument, meaning that either the zero branch $z$ or the successor branch $s$ will be run.
We can thus consider the usage counts of these separately and combine them using the meet operator.
In the zero case, the natural number and the zero branch are both used once giving a combined use of ${\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}$.
In the successor case, the successor branch is used once but the uses of the natural number depends on ${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}$.
When ${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}=0$, it is unused by the successor branch but used once through matching, giving it an affine use.
When ${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}=1$ the natural number is used once through matching, providing the predecessor to be used once and when ${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}=\omega$ it is used an unrestricted number of times.
Summarizing, the uses of the successor branch can be written $(1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}){\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}$ and combined with the zero branch we have $\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}0}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}=((1\wedge{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}){\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}})\wedge({\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}})$
\par If ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=1$, then the recursive call is used once in the step term $s$.
The zero branch will then be used once while the successor branch can be used any number of times.
The uses of the natural number again depends on ${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}$.
When ${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}=0$, it is unused in the successor case but used once through matching, providing a predecessor which is used once through matching in the recursive call.
In total, this gives one use.
When ${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}=1$, the uses from matching are added to the use of the predecessor by the successor branch, giving an unrestricted number of uses in total.
Finally, when ${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}=\omega$, we again have an unrestricted number of uses for the same reason.
These cases can be summarized as $\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}=(1+{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}){\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}+\omega{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}$.
\par If ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=\omega$, then the recursive call is used any number of times in the step term $s$.
In this case, the zero branch, successor branch and the natural number can all be used an unlimited number of times giving a usage count of $\mathsf{nr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\omega}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}=\omega({\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}q_{\mathrm{s}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}})$.
Based on this analysis, we thus propose the following \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Zero-one-many.html#nr, defined by cases on ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}$:
\par\@@amsalign
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{Linear Types.}\ The argument for affine types mostly translates also to linear types.
The only difference is the case ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=0$, ${\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}=1$ where the previously affine use of the natural number in the successor case becomes unrestricted since properly affine uses cannot be expressed anymore.
However, since we defined this case of the $\mathsf{nr}${} function using the meet operator we still get the same \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Zero-one-many.html#nr as for affine types.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{Linear \& Affine Types.}\ This instance combines the affine and linear types instances.
Consequently, the cases ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=0$, ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=1$ and ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=\omega$ are the same as for the linear types instance since the interpretation of the grades are the same.
The remaining case, ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=1^{\text{?}}$ is defined the same as in the affine types instance.
Note that the use of ${\color[rgb]{0.5977,0.3047,0.6406}q_{\mathrm{n}}}$ is affine.
Above this was implicit as this was represented by the unit grade but here it gives an explicit multiplication by $1^{\text{?}}$.
\par Naturally, the analysis done above still applies when the linear and affine types instances are combined.
The cases ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=0$ and ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=\omega$ are the same as above since their interpretations are the same in all three instances.
The cases ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=1$ and ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=1^{\text{?}}$ correspond to the ${\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}r}=1$ cases of their respective instances.
Note that in the latter case, the use of ${\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}q_{\mathrm{z}}}$ is affine and thus multiplied by $1^{\text{?}}$ instead of the (implicit) $1$ in the linear case.
In summary, we get the following \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Linear-or-affine.html#nr:
\par\@@amsalign
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Erased matches}
In our case study on erasure (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:erasure}) we identified two possible sources of erased matches and imposed restrictions to prevent problematic uses of these.
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{letouzey:types02}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} discusses similar issues in the context of extraction for Rocq, as do \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{sozeau:coqErasure}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
\par The first source of erased matches is $\mathsf{emptyrec}_{0}\,A\,t$, which allows a term $t$ to be considered unused or erasable in impossible branches.
Requiring consistent contexts allows such programs to be written but ensures that any impossible branch will never be executed when the program is run.
\par Few other works appear to have investigated (elimination of) the empty type in a graded setting.
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{DBLP:journals/corr/abs-2005-02247}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} do this for a system with simple types but do not allow this style of treating impossible branches as erased.
The usage rule corresponding to their system adds an arbitrary context to that of $t$ instead of scaling with some $p$ as we do.
\par The other source of erased matches is $\mathsf{prodrec}$, which was restricted to disallow matching on erasable pairs in non-erased settings.
Previous work has studied systems both where matching on eliminated pairs \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{DBLP:conf/esop/MoonEO21}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} or forced patterns \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{DBLP:journals/pacmpl/Tejiscak20}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} is allowed and systems where it is not \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{choudhuryEadesEisenbergWeirich:popl21}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, but the consequences of making either choice appear to be less studied.
\par\par Our formalization includes the ability to restrict what values the $r$ annotation on $\mathsf{prodrec}$ is allowed to take.
For erasure, we can thus instantiate our system in two ways, either by allowing $r=0$ or not, giving us the ability to explore the consequences of the choice.
\par As an example of the consequences of allowing or disallowing erased matches for $\mathsf{prodrec}$, consider the following, weaker version of our soundness theorem, \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:soundness}, where only the source language is considered.
\par\inLinkedTheorem[Canonicity]If\/ $\Gamma$ is consistent, $\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}$ and $\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t$ hold, and $\mathsf{Prodrec}\,{0}$ is false, then there is a natural number $n$ such that $\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{s}\underline{n}\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}$.
\endinLinkedTheorem
\par If the requirement about $\mathsf{Prodrec}\,{0}$ is dropped, then the theorem does not hold.
A \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Consequences.Soundness.html#soundness-%E2%84%95-only-source-counterexample%E2%82%81 is our example from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:usage}, $t\coloneq\mathsf{prodrec}{}_{0}^{0}\,\mathbb{N}\,\mathsf{x}_{0}\,\mathsf{zero}$, which does not reduce to a numeral, but for which we have $\epsilon.\Sigma^{0}_{\otimes}\,\mathbb{N}\,\mathbb{N}\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}$ and $\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t$, and the context $\epsilon.\Sigma^{0}_{\otimes}\,\mathbb{N}\,\mathbb{N}$ is provably consistent.
While this shows that canonicity for natural numbers is lost when erased matches are allowed, it is notable that this is \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Consequences.Soundness.html#soundness-%E2%84%95-only-target-not-counterexample%E2%82%81 to canonicity for programs compiled to the target language.
In this example $\mathsf{x}_{0}$ is erased and the program is thus extracted directly to $\mathsf{zero}$.
\par Our choice to disallow erased matches for weak $\Sigma$-types in the case study was driven by provability of the fundamental lemma.
The general proof strategy consists of letting $t$ and $t^{\bullet}$ reduce as much as possible and use \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:LRred}, but this strategy fails since $\mathsf{prodrec}{}_{0}^{q}\,A\,t\,u$ might not reduce even though $(\mathsf{prodrec}{}_{0}^{q}\,A\,t\,u)^{\bullet}$ does.
In fact, the fundamental lemma \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.LogicalRelation.Fundamental.Counterexample.html#negation-of-fundamental-lemma-with-erased-matches%E2%82%81 since there are no neutral elements of type $\mathbb{N}$ in the logical relation.
\par Although the fundamental lemma does not hold, we conjecture that canonicity for natural numbers does hold for the target language even in the presence of erased matches.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Unit type}
The difference between the two kinds of pairs might be familiar from linear logic, where the weak and strong pairs correspond to multiplicative and additive conjunction, respectively.
Consequently, one might expect two kinds of unit types, corresponding to the multiplicative and additive unit of linear logic.
We have thus extended our formalization with such types $\top_{k}$ and unit elements $\star_{k}$.
As with $\Sigma$-types, $k$ can be either $\otimes$ or $\&$ for the weak and strong kinds respectively and we may drop the label where there is no need to differentiate between them.
The weak unit type comes with an eliminator $\mathsf{unitrec}$ in the same style as $\mathsf{prodrec}$.
The strong unit type does not have an eliminator, but instead enjoys $\eta$-equality, similarly to the strong $\Sigma$-type.
The typing and reduction rules for the unit types can be found in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:unitTyping}.
\par\begin{figure}\begin{equation*}\frac{\vdash{}\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash{}\top\mathrel{:}\mathsf{U}}\frac{\vdash{}\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash{}\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}\mathrel{:}\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}}\frac{\Gamma.\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}\vdash{}A\quad\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}\quad\Gamma\vdash{}u\mathrel{:}{A[\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}]}}{\Gamma\vdash{}\mathsf{unitrec}_{p}^{q}\,A\,t\,u\mathrel{:}{A[t]}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\&}}\quad\Gamma\vdash{}u\mathrel{:}\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\&}}}{\Gamma\vdash{}t=u\mathrel{:}\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\&}}}\frac{\Gamma.\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}\vdash{}A=A^{\prime}\quad\Gamma\vdash{}t=t^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}\quad\Gamma\vdash{}u=u^{\prime}\mathrel{:}{A[\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}]}}{\Gamma\vdash{}\mathsf{unitrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,t\,u=\mathsf{unitrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A^{\prime}\,t^{\prime}\,u^{\prime}\mathrel{:}{A[t]}}\frac{\Gamma.\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}\vdash{}A\quad\Gamma\vdash{}u\mathrel{:}{A[\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}]}}{\Gamma\vdash{}\mathsf{unitrec}_{p}^{q}\,A\,\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}\,u=u\mathrel{:}{A[\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}]}}\frac{\Gamma.\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}\vdash{}A\quad\Gamma\vdash{}u\mathrel{:}{A[\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}]}\quad\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow{}t^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}}{\Gamma\vdash{}\mathsf{unitrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,t\,u\longrightarrow{}\mathsf{unitrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,t^{\prime}\,u\mathrel{:}{A[t]}}\frac{\Gamma.\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}\vdash{}A\quad\Gamma\vdash{}u\mathrel{:}{A[\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}]}}{\Gamma\vdash{}\mathsf{unitrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}\,u\longrightarrow{}u\mathrel{:}{A[\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}]}}\end{equation*}\Description{Extensions of the judgements for well-formed types, $\Gamma\vdash{}A$, terms, $\Gamma\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}A$ and term reduction, $\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow{}u\mathrel{:}A$, with the inclusion of unit types.}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{11}}{Extensions of the judgements for well-formed types, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.html#_%E2%8A%A2_, terms, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.html#_%E2%8A%A2_%E2%88%B7_ and term reduction, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.html#_%E2%8A%A2_%E2%87%92_%E2%88%B7_, with the inclusion of unit types.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{Figure 11}}{Extensions of the judgements for well-formed types, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.html#_%E2%8A%A2_, terms, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.html#_%E2%8A%A2_%E2%88%B7_ and term reduction, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.html#_%E2%8A%A2_%E2%87%92_%E2%88%B7_, with the inclusion of unit types.}}}
\end{figure}
\par More interesting is perhaps what usage rules should be assigned to these.
For the type itself we assign the zero usage context $\mathbf{0}$ as we did for the other base types.
For the unit elements we might expect that the rules differ between the two kinds in the same way as in linear logic or as the $\Sigma$-types do.
We take the approach that the unit elements should serve as units for their respective pairs in terms of usage.
That is, terms like $(t,\star_{k})_{k}$ and $t$ should have the same resource usage.
\par For the weak unit type, this means that we assign context $\mathbf{0}$ since it is the identity for addition.
The rule for $\mathsf{unitrec}$ follows the pattern of $\mathsf{prodrec}$, allowing the uses of the scrutinee to be scaled by a given grade annotation corresponding to its number of uses, see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:unitUsage}.
In particular, it allows an erased match where no uses of the unit element are counted.
As for pairs, this should be safe since there is only one constructor but we allow preventing this (and other grade annotations) through a configurable side condition.
\par For the strong unit element, our approach indicates that we would like to assign a context that is the identity for meet.
This would mean that we should assign the greatest usage context but our modalities do not necessarily include a greatest element (the linear types instance is an example).
Instead, we note that due to the idempotence of meet, we could achieve our goal if the usage context assigned to $\star_{\&}$ above is the same as the one assigned to $t$.
We thus allow the strong unit element to be used in any context.
In a sense, $\star_{\&}$ acts as a ``sink'', allowing a program to ``consume'' any variable an arbitrary number of times.
These usage rules are summarized in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:unitUsage}.
Like pairs, the rules for the unit elements matches to those of linear logic \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{linearLogic}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} with the weak unit corresponding to the multiplicative unit ($1$) which can only be derived from no assumptions while the strong unit corresponds to the additive unit ($\top$) which is derivable under any assumptions.
\par\begin{figure}\begin{equation*}\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\top}\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\otimes}}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t\quad{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}u\quad\eta.{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}A}{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}{\color[rgb]{0.3008,0.6875,0.2891}\gamma}+{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.5078,0.75}\delta}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\mathsf{unitrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.2148,0.4922,0.7227}q}}\,A\,t\,u}\;\mathsf{Unitrec}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}\frac{}{\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}\&}}}\end{equation*}\Description{Extensions of the usage relation, $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t$, with the inclusion of unit types.}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{12}}{Extensions of the usage relation, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Usage.html#_%E2%96%B8%5B_%5D_, with the inclusion of unit types.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{Figure 12}}{Extensions of the usage relation, \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Usage.html#_%E2%96%B8%5B_%5D_, with the inclusion of unit types.}}}
\end{figure}
\par A consequence of our usage rule for the strong unit element is that the inference (and decision) procedure for usage we described in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:usage} no longer works as it is based on the syntax containing sufficient grade annotations.
To get the usage rule $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}\star_{\&}$ and still support usage inference, one could perhaps annotate $\star_{\&}$ with the vector of resources to be consumed.
We did not pursue this approach further, as it would seemingly require a large change to our formalization.
In particular, applying substitutions would require the annotated context to change by multiplying by a corresponding substitution matrix.
\par Apart from usage inference, the results we have presented above hold also in the presence of these unit types.
The logical relation for types is extended in the expected way:
\begin{equation*}\frac{\Gamma\vdash{}A\longrightarrow^{*}\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}}{\Gamma\vDash A}\quad\textsc{left={$\langle\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}\rangle$},vcenter}\end{equation*}
Similarly, the target language is extended with a unit element and eliminator and corresponding reduction rules (see the formalization for details).
The extraction function is updated as follows:
\begin{equation*}\top^{\bullet}\coloneqq\hbox to12.78pt{\vbox to11.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5898,0.2734,0.2734}\lxSVG@setdash{0.5pt,1.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.35 0.35 L 0.35 19.03 L 16.95 19.03 L 16.95 0.35 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\star^{\bullet}\coloneq\star\mathsf{unitrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}0}}^{q}\,A\,t\,u^{\bullet}\coloneq u^{\bullet}\mathsf{unitrec}_{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}\omega}}^{q}\,A\,t\,u^{\bullet}\coloneq\mathsf{unitrec}{}\,t^{\bullet}\,u^{\bullet}\end{equation*}
Matches on erased unit elements are thus removed, similarly to matches on erased pairs.
\par The erasure logical relation is extended such that $t\mathrel{\circledR}v\mathrel{:}A\mathbin{/}\langle\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}\rangle$ holds iff $\Delta_{0}\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{*}\star_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}\mathrel{:}\top_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}k}}$ and $v\longrightarrow^{*}\star$, ensuring that both terms reduce to unit elements.
The fundamental lemma and soundness of erasure can be proven as before with the caveat that erased matches for $\mathsf{unitrec}$ should be disallowed where it was disallowed for $\mathsf{prodrec}$.
The discussion about erased matches for $\mathsf{prodrec}$ in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:erasedMatching} applies also to $\mathsf{unitrec}$.
In particular, one can construct a similar \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Consequences.Soundness.html#soundness-%E2%84%95-only-source-counterexample%E2%82%85 to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:soundness} when erased matches for $\mathsf{unitrec}$ are allowed.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Information flow interpretation}
The erasure lattice $\omega\leq 0$ also serves as a two-point
information flow lattice $\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{H}$ where publicly
available resources are labelled with $\mathsf{L}$ and private (confidential)
resources are labelled with $\mathsf{H}$.
A program $t$ is viewed from the public perspective, and
a context $\gamma$ with $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}t$ describes how it accesses its resources
(its free variables).
The law $\mathsf{L}+\mathsf{H}=\mathsf{L}$ expresses that a resource that is accessed both
publicly and privately must be publicly available.
Our fundamental lemma for the erasure logical relation (\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:fundamental}) implies a form of \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Consequences.Non-interference.html#non-interference:
the result of computing a natural number does not depend on the values of $\mathsf{H}$-labelled resources.
\par In a two-sided formulation $\gamma\Gamma\vdash t:pA$ one can view
programs from perspectives $p$ other than $\mathsf{L}=1$.
Depending on what rules are used one may have that a variable $x$ can be accessed if and only if $\gamma(x)\leq p$.
For $p=\mathsf{H}$ this means that any variable can be accessed.
\par We can simulate the two-sided approach by shifting the perspective
from $1$ to $p$ by \emph{dividing} $\gamma$ by $p$.
This happens pointwise, so $(\gamma/p)(x)=\gamma(x)/p$.
Let us say that a modality structure \emph{supports division by $q$} if there is a function $-/q$ such that the Galois connection $\forall p,r.\ p/q\leq r\iff p\leq q\cdot r$ holds.
Note that $p/q$ is the \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Properties.Division.html#%2F-least-%E2%89%A4%C2%B7 $r$ such that $p\leq q\cdot r$, and that $-/q$ is \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Properties.Division.html#%2F-monotone%CB%A1%E2%80%B2.
The erasure instance supports division by $\mathsf{L}$, with $p/\mathsf{L}=p$, and by $\mathsf{H}$, with $p/\mathsf{H}=\mathsf{L}$.
The latter equality entails that all resources are accessible from a private perspective.
\par This shift of perspective can be carried out in finer information flow lattices.
Recall that bounded, distributive lattices with decidable equality can be seen as modality structures where $1$ is the least element, $0$ is the greatest element, addition is meet, and multiplication is join.
One example of such a lattice is the total order \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Information-flow.html#L%E2%89%A4M%E2%89%A4H,
where we have added a \emph{medium} clearance to the low and high ones.
It is straightforward to derive the following division laws in such lattices:
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Properties.Division.html#%2F%F0%9D%9F%99%E2%89%A1 and
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Properties.Division.html#%2F%F0%9D%9F%98%E2%89%A1%F0%9D%9F%99 and
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Properties.Division.html#%2F%E2%89%A1%F0%9D%9F%99 and
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Properties.Division.html#%F0%9D%9F%99%2F%E2%89%A1%F0%9D%9F%99 and
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Properties.Division.html#%F0%9D%9F%98%2F%E2%89%A1%F0%9D%9F%98.
The last three are given under the assumption that division by $p$ is supported, and for the last one we additionally assume that $p\not=0$ and that the zero-product property holds.
For the lattice $\mathsf{L}\leq\mathsf{M}\leq\mathsf{H}$ we get that $\mathsf{L}/\mathsf{M}=\mathsf{L}$ and $\mathsf{M}/\mathsf{M}=\mathsf{L}$ and $\mathsf{H}/\mathsf{M}=\mathsf{H}$, so shifting the perspective to $\mathsf{M}$ makes $\mathsf{M}$-labelled resources available.
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Extension: modes and graded \texorpdfstring{$\Sigma$}{Σ}-types}
Let us now present an extension of the theory with support for
\emph{graded Σ-types}:
The type $\Sigma^{q}_{k,p}\,F\,G$ is a variant of
$\Sigma^{q}_{k}\,F\,G$ where the first component, of type $F$, has
grade $p$.
If the erasure instance is used then $\Sigma^{q}_{k,0}\,F\,G$
is a $\Sigma$-type with an erased first component.
This kind of type can be used to have an erased component, such as a
proof of an invariant, in a data structure.
\par Note that it might not make sense to apply $\mathsf{fst}$ to a value of
type, say, $\Sigma^{0}_{\&,0}\,\mathbb{N}\,\mathbb{N}$: if the first
component is erased then there is nothing to return.
So, in order to support graded $\Sigma$-types we follow
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} and switch to a usage relation with one
of two \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Mode.html#Mode: $0_{\textsf{M}}$ (erased/compile-time) or $1_{\textsf{M}}$
(present/run-time).
The idea is that $\mathsf{fst}$ is allowed for the graded $\Sigma$-type
$\Sigma^{q}_{\&,0}\,F\,G$ only if the mode is $0_{\textsf{M}}$,
whereas there are no extra restrictions for
$\Sigma^{q}_{\&,p}\,F\,G$ when $p\neq 0$.
\par In this section we work with modality structures with well-behaved zeros
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:well-behaved-zero}), i.e., we have $0\neq 1$,
addition, meet, and $\mathsf{nr}$ are positive, and the zero-product property holds.
We require that $0\neq 1$ to ensure that the modes $0_{\textsf{M}}$ and
$1_{\textsf{M}}$ correspond to different grades.
\par As mentioned above, the instances for erasure, affine types, linear
types, and linear or affine types discussed in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:modalities}
all have well-behaved zeros, and we have proved a subject reduction
lemma for arbitrary such instances
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:subject-reduction-modes}).
However, note that our main soundness theorem
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:soundness-modes}) is focused on erasure.
Thus, we cannot claim that the definitions below make sense in all contexts
for all instances with well-behaved zeros, we just use erasure to
motivate the definitions.
\par Modes can be \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Mode.html#%E2%8C%9C_%E2%8C%9D grades: $\overline{0_{\textsf{M}}}=0$
and $\overline{1_{\textsf{M}}}=1$.
Grades can also be
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Mode.html#%E2%8C%9E_%E2%8C%9F to modes
($\underline{0}=0_{\textsf{M}}$, and $\underline{p}=1_{\textsf{M}}$ if
$p\neq 0$), and modes can be
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Mode.html#_%E1%B5%90%C2%B7_ by values
of type $\mathcal{M}$ ($0_{\textsf{M}}\odot p=0_{\textsf{M}}$ and
$1_{\textsf{M}}\odot p=\underline{p}$).
\par\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Untyped.html#Term is modified a
little to support $\Sigma^{q}_{k,p}\,F\,G$: the projections
$\mathsf{fst}$ and $\mathsf{snd}$, the pair constructor, and $\mathsf{prodrec}$
are all annotated with a grade corresponding to $p$ (in addition to
any prior annotations).
The \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.html#%E2%8A%A2_ and \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Definition.Typed.html#_%E2%8A%A2_%E2%87%92_%E2%88%B7_ are mostly unchanged, except to
ensure that the $p$ annotations match when appropriate---see the
formalization for details.
However, the usage relation is modified substantially.
Its definition is inspired by the type system presented by
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
\par\inLinkedDefinition[Moded grading]Given a usage context $\gamma$, a mode $m$ and a term $t$, we say
that $t$ is \emph{well-resourced} with respect to $\gamma$ and $m$
if $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{m}t$, where the relation $\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}$ is
defined inductively in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:moded-grading}.
\endinLinkedDefinition
\begin{figure}\begin{equation*}\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}\mathsf{U}}\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}\mathbb{N}}\ignorespaces\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}\bot}\frac{\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}\odot{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}F\quad\delta.\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}q\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}G}{p\gamma+\delta\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}\Pi^{q}_{p}\,F\,G}\frac{\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}\odot{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}F\quad\delta.\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}q\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}G}{{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}\gamma+\delta\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}\Sigma^{q}_{k,{\color[rgb]{0.7383,0.1016,0.1094}p}}\,F\,G}\frac{}{\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}\mathbf{e}_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}\mathsf{x}_{i}}\frac{\gamma.\overline{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}p\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}t}{\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}\lambda^{p}t}\frac{\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}}t\quad\delta\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0}m}\odot{\c grading $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{m}t$. occurrences are highlighted in colour.}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{13}}{Moded grading \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Usage.html#_%E2%96%B8%5B_%5D_. New variable

\end{figure}
\par The variable rule uses the usage context $\overline{m}\mathbf{e}_{i}$.
If $m$ is $1_{\textsf{M}}$, then we get the same context as before, but if
$m$ is $0_{\textsf{M}}$, then we get
$\overline{0_{\textsf{M}}}\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathbf{0}$, so the variable is
not counted.
In the antecedent of the rule for $\lambda$-abstractions the grade of
variable $0$ is $\overline{m}p$: if $m$ is $0_{\textsf{M}}$, then
$\overline{m}p=0$, which matches the variable rule's grade
context for $0_{\textsf{M}}$.
Multiplication by $\overline{m}$ is used in this way also in other
rules.
\par Note that $m\odot p$ is $0_{\textsf{M}}$ exactly when
$m=0_{\textsf{M}}$ or $p=0$.
This construction is used when we want to ensure that things are
checked in the erased mode $0_{\textsf{M}}$ if either the mode is already
$0_{\textsf{M}}$, or some argument is erased, for instance for
$\Pi^{q}_{0}\,F\,G$, $\Sigma^{q}_{k,0}\,F\,G$ and
$t\,^{0}u$.
\par The usage rules given above are partly based on Agda's rules for
erasure \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{abel-danielsson-vezzosi-erased-univalence}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
Agda supports cubical type theory, which imposes restrictions on what
rules one can use for erasure (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:also-in-types}).
We have tried to be compatible with the rules of Agda.
In particular, the rules for $\Pi$ and $\Sigma$ are based on those of
Agda.
In Agda $p$ and $q$ are required to be equal, and the formalization
has a parameter which makes it possible to enforce this requirement.
\par The term $\mathsf{fst}{}_{p}\,t$ can only be used if the mode is
$m\odot p$, so if $p=0$, then the mode must be
$0_{\textsf{M}}$.
There is also a side condition: if $m\odot p$ is $1$, then
$p\leq 1$ must hold.
This condition is used in the proof of
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:subject-reduction-modes}.
Note that for the erasure, affine types, linear types, and linear or
affine types modalities, the only grade that is not bounded by $1$ is
$0$.
\par The first component of a pair of type $\Sigma^{q}_{k,p}\,F\,G$
is associated with a grade $p$, and correspondingly the rules for the
pair constructors include scaling by $p$ in their conclusions.
Scaling by $p$ is also used in the rule for $\mathsf{prodrec}$: note that
if $p$ is $0$, then there must be no (counted) uses of the variable
corresponding to the first component in $u$.
\par The rules for $\mathsf{prodrec}$, $\mathsf{natrec}$ and $\mathsf{emptyrec}$ still
include assumptions about the motives (``$A$''), but now these
assumptions use the mode $0_{\textsf{M}}$, and in the case of
$\mathsf{prodrec}$ and $\mathsf{natrec}$ the last grade in the usage context
is $0$: the grade $q$ is no longer used.
Note that $\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{0_{\textsf{M}}}A$ holds
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Usage.Restrictions.Satisfied.html#%F0%9D%9F%98%E1%B6%9C%E2%96%B8%5B%F0%9D%9F%98%E1%B5%90%5D%E2%87%94
when $\mathsf{Prodrec}\,{r}$ holds for all subterms in $A$ of the form
$\mathsf{prodrec}{}_{r,p}^{q}\,B\,t\,u$.
\par\par\par We can prove a substitution lemma for the theory with graded $\Sigma$-types,
and using that we obtain the following variant of subject reduction
for usage:
\inLinkedTheorem[Moded subject reduction]If $\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{m}t$ and $\Gamma\vdash{}t\longrightarrow{}u\mathrel{:}A$ then
$\gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{m}u$.
\endinLinkedTheorem
\par The extraction function from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:erasure} is
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Erasure.Extraction.html#erase for some term
formers corresponding to graded $\Sigma$-types.
Erased first components are erased entirely, and $\mathsf{fst}{}_{0}\,t$ is
replaced by $\mathord{\circlearrowleft}$.
Uses of $\mathsf{prodrec}$ with pairs with an erased first component are extracted to $(\lambda\,{u^{\bullet}[{\Uparrow}\mathord{\circlearrowleft}]})\,t^{\bullet}$ which substitutes $\mathord{\circlearrowleft}$ for the first component and then applies $t^{\bullet}$ to be used as the second component.
The updated cases are as follows (here $\omega$ stands for any grade distinct
from $0$):

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.18057pt}{5.62498pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 15.57 L 39.24 15.57 L 39.24 0 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.69 0.69 L 0.69 14.87 L 38.55 14.87 L 38.55 0.69 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.2734,0.5898,0.2734}\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.35 0.35 L 0.35 15.22 L 38.9 15.22 L 38.9 0.35 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.5pt}{4.44444pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{59}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}} backgrounds refer to the strict and non-strict variants of the target language respectively.
Note that in the strict case, lambdas are used to construct a strict pair in the sense that both its components are evaluated to values.
\par With these definitions in place we can state the main soundness
theorem for the theory with graded $\Sigma$-types (the statement is identical
to that of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:soundness}).
We do not include a proof here but refer to the formalization for
details.
\inLinkedTheorem[Operational erasure soundness]Let $\Delta$ be a context.
If\/ $\mathsf{Prodrec}\,{0}$ is false or $\Delta$ is empty,
$\Delta$ is consistent if $\mathsf{Emptyrec}\,{0}$ is true,
and furthermore
$\Delta\vdash{}t\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}$ and $\mathbf{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleright$}}^{1_{\textsf{M}}}t$, then
there is a natural number $n$ such that
$\Delta\vdash{}t\longrightarrow^{s*}\underline{n}\mathrel{:}\mathbb{N}$ and $t^{\bullet}\longrightarrow^{s*}\underline{n}$.
\endinLinkedTheorem
\par Note that the reduction relation does not include $\eta$-expansion.
We can prove that there is a type- and
resource-preserving procedure that takes a well-typed, well-resourced
term to one of its $\eta$-long normal forms.
However, there are some restrictions.
The linear identity function $\lambda^{1}x.x$ of type
$\Pi^{r}_{1}\,(\Sigma^{q}_{\&,p}\,\mathbb{N}\,\mathbb{N})\,(\Sigma^{q}_{\&,p}\,\mathbb{N}\,\mathbb{N})$
is definitionally equal to the $\eta$-long normal form
$\lambda^{1}x.(^{p}\mathsf{fst}{}_{p}\,x,\mathsf{snd}{}_{p}\,x)_{\&}$, which is
well-resourced in the empty context
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Reduction.html#%CE%B7-long-nf-for-id%E2%87%94%E2%89%A1%F0%9D%9F%99%E2%8A%8E%E2%89%A1%F0%9D%9F%98 $p=1$ or $1\leq 0=p$.
We have \hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.FullReduction.html#fullRedTerm that
well-typed, well-resourced terms have $\eta$-long normal forms if the
theory is restricted so that, if $\Sigma^{q}_{\&,p}$
is allowed, then $p=1$ or $1\leq 0=p$.
For the
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Erasure.Properties.html#full-reduction-assumptions
instance these conditions always hold, without any restrictions.
For the instance of
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Affine.html#full-reduction-assumptions the conditions hold if
$\Sigma^{q}_{\&,\omega}$ is disallowed.
And finally the conditions hold for the instances of
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Linearity.html#full-reduction-assumptions and
\hrefhttps://graded-type-theory.github.io/graded-type-theory/jfp25/Graded.Modality.Instances.Linear-or-affine.html#full-reduction-assumptions types if $\Sigma^{q}_{\&,p}$
is only allowed when $p=1$.
Note that there are no restrictions for
$\Sigma^{q}_{\otimes,p}$.
\par The graded $\Sigma$-types discussed here are similar to the tensor products
presented by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, but there are some
differences.
\cite[citeauthor]{\@@bibref{Authors}{DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18}{}{}} has a single tensor product/$\Sigma$-type
with a single usage annotation.
In his theory the projections $\mathsf{fst}$ and $\mathsf{snd}$ are allowed
only in the erased fragment (when the mode is $0_{\textsf{M}}$), whereas a
construction similar to $\mathsf{prodrec}$ is always allowed (but there is
nothing like the ``third grade $r$'', and erased matches are not
supported).
The usual $\eta$-equality rule for $\Sigma$-types is available for tensor products
only in the erased fragment: \cite[citeauthor]{\@@bibref{Authors}{DBLP:conf/lics/Atkey18}{}{}} does
not separate usage from typing, so it is straightforward to restrict
an equality rule in this way.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusions}
We have presented a dependently typed core language with grades
for variable usage, generic over a semiring-like modality structure. The language
features $\Pi$, weak and strong $\Sigma$, a universe, an empty type,
and an infinite base type with induction. We have formalized
the meta-theory up to normalization and decidability of definitional
equality in Agda, including standard results such as substitution
lemmata, subject reduction, and consistency, building on the work of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{DBLP:journals/pacmpl/0001OV18}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}. We further added a usage
calculus and showed its correctness via a subject reduction result.
As an instance of our generic usage calculus, we studied modality structures able to track erasure and proved their soundness with respect to program extraction by a logical relations argument.
\par Some loose ends that could be investigated in future work:
\begin{itemize}\par\itemize@item@Utilize the formalization to implement a verified type checker for graded modal type theory. This type checker would be provably terminating and produce (erased) derivations of well-typedness.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Prove that extraction is sound also in the presence of erased
matches for weak $\Sigma$-types (and weak unit type).
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Further investigation on grade assignment for natural numbers, or recursive types more generally.
While one could presumably extend the theory with ``$\mathsf{nr}${}-like'' functions for the types one wants to add, it might not be clear what requirements such functions would need to satisfy to ensure correct grade assignment.
As discussed above, the same can be said also for our $\mathsf{nr}${} functions.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Add grade inference, following
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{DBLP:journals/pacmpl/Tejiscak20}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
A full formalization might require a lot of work, but one could work
against an abstract constraint solver whose correctness is
stipulated.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Investigate the interaction of modalities with meta-variables
used for type reconstruction in real-world dependently typed
languages such as Agda and Idris.
Implementations already exist, but theoretical work is still lacking.
\par\end{itemize}
